Conditional thinning and multiplicative statistics of Laguerre-type orthogonal polynomial ensembles
Leslie Molag, Guilherme L. F. Silva, Lun Zhang

Abstract
. We study the local statistics of orthogonal polynomial ensembles near a hard edge, subject to a multiplicative deformation of the measure. Probabilistically, this deformation corresponds to a position-dependent conditional thinning of the particles. We prove that, under critical hard edge scaling and for a large class of potentials and deformation symbols, the correlation kernel of the conditional ensemble converges to a universal limit, which we identify as the conditional thinned Bessel point process. We derive an explicit expression for this limiting kernel in terms of the solution to a nonlocal integrable system depending on a parameter. For a special choice of the parameter, this system was recently identified in the study of multiplicative statistics of the Bessel point process. Our results establish that this system governs the full correlation structure of the conditional Bessel point process, extending the classical connection between the standard Bessel kernel and the Painlevé V equation.



1.
Introduction

The universality of spectral statistics is a cornerstone of Random Matrix Theory (RMT). From its origins in nuclear physics to modern applications in number theory, statistical mechanics, and high-dimensional data analysis, the local correlation functions of eigenvalues have proven to be robust invariants across a vast array of models, and a rich ground for the prediction of new critical phenomena.

Among the many striking connections between random particle systems and other fields, the link to integrable systems has proven exceptionally fruitful. This relationship is most celebrated at the “soft edge” of the spectrum, that is, close to the largest eigenvalue. The fluctuations of the largest eigenvalues in Hermitian random matrix models are described by the Airy
2
point process, and the distribution of the largest particle is the Tracy-Widom distribution
F_{2}
. In turn,
F_{2}
is governed by the Hastings-McLeod solution to the Painlevé II equation, a second order nonlinear integrable ODE. Such a connection between extremal eigenvalues and nonlinear differential equations was pioneered by Tracy and Widom
[
53
]
and Forrester
[
36
]
for the Gaussian Unitary Ensemble (GUE). These results were subsequently extended to a wide variety of random matrix models using diverse techniques
[
44
,
31
,
27
,
28
,
51
]
.

But the connection between strongly correlated particle systems and integrable systems extends well beyond RMT. The distribution
F_{2}
describes the fluctuations of the longest increasing subsequences in random permutations
[
5
,
45
]
and, more generally, characterizes the universal scaling limit of growth models in the Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ) universality class
[
3
,
48
]
.

In recent years, attention has expanded from standard gap probabilities to the study of multiplicative statistics of these point processes. It was discovered that the generating functions for multiplicative statistics of the Airy
2
process relate directly to the solution of the KPZ equation
[
3
,
48
,
9
,
17
,
33
]
, and that the underlying point process, when weighted by these statistics, forms a new “conditional” ensemble with a rich integrable structure
[
21
,
13
,
12
]
. Furthermore, this framework has become a powerful tool for extracting fine-grained probabilistic data, such as large deviation estimates and tail asymptotics
[
24
,
13
,
12
,
55
]
, while also motivating recent studies on higher-order soft edge scalings
[
16
,
10
,
15
,
11
,
20
,
26
,
41
]
.

The developments mentioned so far concern RMT fluctuations around a so-called “soft edge”, and a parallel and equally rich structure exists at the “hard edge” of the spectrum. A hard edge arises when eigenvalues are bounded by a strict physical constraint, most commonly the origin for positive definite matrices. The canonical model for this regime is the Wishart ensemble (or Laguerre Unitary Ensemble - LUE), consisting of matrices of the form
XX^{*}
, where
X
is a rectangular matrix with complex Gaussian entries
[
58
]
. As the matrix size grows, the eigenvalues near the origin, when critically scaled, converge to the Bessel point process, a canonical point process whose correlations are described by Bessel functions
[
36
,
39
]
.

Fluctuations of eigenvalues near a hard edge also display a rich structure in connection with integrable systems.
Historically, the gap probabilities for the Bessel process were connected to the Painlevé V equation by Tracy and Widom
[
52
]
, see also
[
43
]
. This connection was further extended to generating functions and multi-interval statistics over the years
[
25
,
4
,
19
]
. However, in light of the recent developments at the soft edge, it is natural to ask: To what extent does the rich integrable structure of multiplicative statistics carry over at a hard edge?

In the present work, we provide an affirmative answer to this question by explicitly constructing the kernel of a limiting point process associated with these multiplicative statistics, and describing it in terms of integrable systems.

We begin by considering the eigenvalues of positive definite matrices of size
n
. We subject these eigenvalues to a conditional thinning procedure, where each eigenvalue is retained or removed independently with a position-dependent probability, and the final process is the resulting ensemble conditioned that all particles have been retained. This procedure defines a finite-
n
conditional ensemble. Our main result describes the asymptotic behavior, as
n\to\infty
, of the conditional ensemble in the critical regime where eigenvalues accumulate near the hard edge.

We prove that, under the hard-edge scaling, the correlation kernel of the finite conditional ensemble converges to a universal limit. Our results are universal, in the sense that we establish them for a broad class of random matrix models with a hard edge, as well as for a wide variety of thinning profiles. We identify this limit as the conditional thinned Bessel point process, which corresponds to applying the conditional thinning operation directly to the classical Bessel point process. Furthermore, our asymptotic analysis yields an explicit formula for the correlation kernel of this process in terms of the solution to a nonlocal integrable system.

The integrable structure of multiplicative statistics for the Bessel point process was recently investigated by Ruzza
[
46
]
, who showed that they are governed by a specific nonlocal integrable equation, which recovers the classical Painlevé V equation in the degenerate limit of gap probabilities. In our analysis, we explicitly identify the nonlocal system characterizing our limiting kernel with the equation derived by Ruzza. This identification is crucial: it shows that the solution to this nonlocal equation describes not just the multiplicative statistics, but the full correlation kernel of the conditional ensemble itself. Thus, the nonlocal Painlevé V transcendent emerges as the fundamental structural invariant of the conditionally thinned Bessel process.

This result completes the description of conditional thinning statistics across the standard spectral regimes. The kernel for the conditional thinning ensemble was related to nonlocal integrable systems at a regular soft edge in
[
37
]
, and at a regular bulk point in
[
18
]
(see also
[
22
]
for further discussion, and
[
12
,
23
]
for related multiplicative statistics). Our work establishes the analogous theory for regular hard edges.

Our analysis relies on the integrable structure of the ensembles via orthogonal polynomials (OPs) and the associated Riemann-Hilbert Problem (RHP) approach. The conditional thinned point process of an orthogonal polynomial ensemble is itself an orthogonal polynomial ensemble, defined by a multiplicative deformation of the original weight
[
21
]
. This observation serves as the starting point for our RHP formulation of the finite
n
correlation kernel.

While we apply the Deift-Zhou steepest descent method, our analysis deviates from the standard route in the construction of the local parametrix near the hard edge. Unlike the classical case, the jump matrices for this local parametrix cannot be reduced to piecewise constant matrices. Consequently, the resulting model problem involves nontrivial position-dependent jumps that encode the symbol of the deformation. This model problem requires a separate asymptotic analysis, and ultimately it establishes the connection with the integrable systems that emerge.

Finally, armed with the asymptotics for the conditional correlation kernel, we recover the multiplicative statistics using a recently established general deformation formula
[
38
]
. This approach bypasses the traditional route using differential identities for Hankel determinants.

2.
Statement of results

The general family of models we consider consists of deformations of the Laguerre-type OP ensembles. More precisely, we consider random points
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in(0,\infty)

with joint distribution
of the form

(2.1)

\frac{1}{\mathsf{Z}_{n}(\mathsf{s})}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\omega_{n}(x_{j}\mid\mathsf{s})\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}x_{n},

where

(2.2)

\omega_{n}(x)=\omega_{n}(x\mid\mathsf{s})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sigma_{n}(x)x^{\alpha}\ee^{-nV(x)},\quad\alpha>-1,\quad x>0,

with

(2.3)

\sigma_{n}(x)=\sigma_{n}^{Q}(x\mid\mathsf{s})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-n^{2m}Q(x)}},\quad\mathsf{s}\in\mathbb{R},\quad m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\text{ fixed},

and the normalization constant

\mathsf{Z}_{n}(\mathsf{s})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\cdots\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{j<k}(x_{k}-x_{j})^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\omega_{n}(x_{j}\mid\mathsf{s})\mathrm{d}x_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}x_{n},

also known as the partition function. In the formula above, the function
\sigma_{n}
should be seen as a deformation factor, and
\omega_{n}
is thus viewed as a deformation of the weight
x^{\alpha}\ee^{-nV(x)}
. We refer to
\sigma_{n}
as the symbol of the deformation, depending parametrically on
\mathsf{s}\in\mathbb{R}
. Further conditions on
\sigma_{n}
will be placed, and it is in fact more convenient to place these conditions on the function
Q
itself. We comment more on the meaning of these quantities in a moment.

Still about (
2.2
), we assume that
V
satisfies the following conditions.

Assumption 2.1
.

The potential
V
in (
2.2
) is a polynomial with positive leading coefficient, and is such that the equilibrium measure
\mathrm{d}\mu_{V}(x)=\mu^{\prime}_{V}(x)\mathrm{d}x
over the interval
[0,+\infty)
in the external field
V
is supported on a single interval
[0,a]
. Moreover, we assume that
\mu_{V}
is regular with a (regular) hard edge at
x=0
and a (regular) soft edge at
x=a
.

The measure
\mu_{V}
is the limiting global distribution of particles of the model (
2.1
) as
n\to\infty
. When
\sigma_{n}\equiv 1
, this is a known result, and for
\sigma_{n}
as considered here this result is a consequence of our analysis.

For the reader unfamiliar with potential theory, we introduce and discuss the equilibrium measure
\mu_{V}
in a more detailed manner in Section
6.1
. The regularity conditions on
\mu_{V}
that we work with are the analogue of the traditional one-cut regular assumptions for equilibrium measures on
\mathbb{R}
, and are standard in random matrix theory. We recall them in detail in Assumption
6.1
below. This regularity holds for a large family of potentials
V
, for instance when
V
is convex, the case
V(x)=x
(the Laguerre weight) being the prototypical example. The most relevant part for the coming discussion is the regularity assumption at the hard edge
x=0
: this condition means that the density
\mu^{\prime}_{V}
of
\mu_{V}
blows up as a square-root at the origin,

(2.4)

\mu^{\prime}_{V}(x)=\kappa_{0}|x|^{-1/2}(1+\mathit{o}(1)),\quad x\searrow 0,\quad\text{ for some constant }\kappa_{0}>0.

Our focus in the current paper is in describing the effect of the deformation
\sigma_{n}
on eigenvalues near
x=0
, that is, near the (regular) hard edge. As such, many of our local scaling limits will still hold if we drop the regularity condition on the soft edge
x=a
, or also if we drop the assumption that
\supp\mu_{V}
is connected. Nevertheless, we work under such conditions to simplify our analysis, as our main goal is not to work under the most general assumptions, but instead to compute the novel scaling limits that arise at the hard edge, and showcase their universality.

By (
2.3
), it is readily seen that
\sigma_{n}(x)\to 1
as
\mathsf{s}\to+\infty
, and (
2.1
) reduces to the classical Laguerre-type OP ensemble. From now on, whenever we refer to the classical Laguerre-type OP ensemble, we always mean (
2.1
) with the choice
\mathsf{s}=+\infty
, that is,
\sigma_{n}\equiv 1
. With this degeneration in mind, the quotient

(2.5)

\mathsf{L}_{n}^{Q}(\mathsf{s})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\mathsf{Z}_{n}(\mathsf{s})}{\mathsf{Z}_{n}(+\infty)}=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{n}(x_{j}\mid\mathsf{s})\right]

is a multiplicative statistic of the classical Laguerre-type OP ensemble, associated to the symbol
{\sigma_{n}(x\mid\mathsf{s})}
.

Alongside the interest in the multiplicative statistics
\mathsf{L}_{n}^{Q}(\mathsf{s})
, the model (
2.1
) may also be interpreted as a deformed ensemble obtained when performing a conditional thinning procedure. Starting from the classical Laguerre-type OP ensemble with
n
particles
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
, each particle receives a mark
1
with probability
\sigma_{n}(x_{j})
, and a mark
0
with complementary probability
1-\sigma_{n}(x_{j})
. The mark
1
is interpreted as if the marked particle is “seen” in the system, and the mark
0
as if the particle is “lost” by noise in the sample. This marking interpretation corresponds to the thinning step. Now construct a new particle system, the conditional thinned ensemble, obtained by conditioning that all the particles received the mark
1
. A general framework developed by Claeys and Glesner
[
21
]
shows that (
2.1
) is precisely the density of particles in this conditional thinned ensemble starting from the corresponding Laguerre-type OP ensemble.

With the conditional thinned interpretation we just explained in mind, it is natural to expect that statistics of (
2.1
) may depend on properties of the function
Q
labelling
\sigma_{n}
.
We assume the function
Q:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies the following conditions.

Assumption 2.2
.

The function
Q
extends to an analytic function in a complex neighborhood of
[0,\infty)
such that

(2.6)

Q(x)>0,\qquad x>0,

and

(2.7)

Q(x)=\begin{cases}\mathsf{t}x^{m}(1+\mathcal{O}(x)),&x\to 0,\\
\mathcal{O}(x^{\epsilon}),&x\to\infty,\end{cases}

for the same integer value
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
appearing in (
2.3
), where
\mathsf{t}
and
\epsilon
are some positive numbers.

The scaling
n^{2m}
in
\sigma_{n}
is motivated by the following reasoning. Under our assumptions on
V
, the local statistics of the classical Laguerre-type OP ensemble near the hard edge
z=0
fluctuate on the scale
\mathcal{O}(n^{-2})
. Thus, in a local coordinate
\zeta\approx n^{2}x
the statistics in the
\zeta
-plane should behave as
\mathcal{O}(1)
, that is, the variable
\zeta
now detects the nontrivial fluctuations. Still with this same scaling, it follows from (
2.7
) that in the variable
\zeta
we have
Q(x)=\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{m}n^{-2m})
and therefore

\frac{1}{1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-n^{2m}Q(x)}}\approx\frac{1}{1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-\mathsf{t}\zeta^{m}}}.

That is, with the scaling
n^{2m}
in
\sigma_{n}
and the vanishing condition (
2.7
) in place, the factor
\sigma_{n}
in the deformed ensemble (
2.1
) produces a nontrivial change of behavior in the fluctuations at the local scale
\zeta
, when compared to the classical Laguerre-type OP ensemble.

The positivity condition on
Q
is motivated by the following reasoning. Pointwise, the fact that
Q>0
implies that

\sigma_{n}(x)\to 1,\quad n\to\infty,\quad x>0.

This means that
\sigma_{n}
does not affect the global asymptotics of the random matrix model in intervals of the form
[\alpha,\infty)
with
\alpha>0
, and only in a scale
x=\mathcal{O}(n^{-2})
the statistics of the model should become affected. In principle, this global positivity condition on
(0,\infty)
could be replaced by positivity enforced only near the hard edge
x=0
, at the cost of more complicated analysis near points outside the hard edge. As we mentioned earlier, since our goal is to showcase novel phenomena near the hard edge, we opt for keeping this condition for the sake of simplicity.

Our main results explain these heuristic interpretations rigorously, computing all the limiting quantities in terms of integrable systems. To state our results, we introduce certain quantities that allow us to decode statistics of (
2.1
).

The monic OPs
\mathsf{P}_{j}(x)=\mathsf{P}_{j}^{(n)}(x\mid\mathsf{s})=x^{j}+\cdots
,
j=0,1,2,\ldots
, for the weight
\omega_{n}(\cdot\mid\mathsf{s})
are uniquely determined by

\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathsf{P}_{j}(x)x^{k}\omega_{n}(x\mid\mathsf{s})\mathrm{d}x=0,\quad k=0,\ldots,j-1.

The associated norming constants
\gamma_{j}(\mathsf{s})=\gamma_{j}^{(n)}(\mathsf{s})>0
, determined from

(2.8)

\frac{1}{\gamma_{j}(\mathsf{s})^{2}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathsf{P}_{j}(x)^{2}\omega_{n}(x\mid\mathsf{s})\mathrm{d}x,

are such that the sequence
\{\gamma_{j}(\mathsf{s})\mathsf{P}_{j},j=0,1,2,\ldots\}
is orthonormal in
L^{2}(\omega_{n}(x\mid\mathsf{s})\mathrm{d}x,[0,\infty))
. With these quantities in mind, we introduce the Christoffel-Darboux kernel

(2.9)

\mathsf{K}_{n}(x,y)=\mathsf{K}_{n}(x,y\mid\mathsf{s})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\gamma_{j}(\mathsf{s})^{2}\mathsf{P}_{j}(x)\mathsf{P}_{j}(y),

and correlation kernel

(2.10)

\widehat{\mathsf{K}}_{n}(x,y)=\widehat{\mathsf{K}}_{n}(x,y\mid\mathsf{s})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\omega_{n}(x\mid\mathsf{s})^{1/2}\mathsf{K}_{n}(x,y\mid\mathsf{s})\omega_{n}(y\mid\mathsf{s})^{1/2}.

All the quantities we just introduced depend smoothly on the parameter
\mathsf{s}
, although sometimes we do not make explicit mention to it in our notations. Also, as we will show, the limiting quantities

\gamma_{j}^{(n)}(\infty)=\lim_{\mathsf{s}\to+\infty}\gamma_{j}^{(n)}(\mathsf{s})\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{\mathsf{K}}_{n}(\cdot\mid\infty)=\lim_{\mathsf{s}\to+\infty}\widehat{\mathsf{K}}_{n}(\cdot\mid\mathsf{s})

make sense, and are the norming constants and correlation kernel for the undeformed weight
x^{\alpha}\ee^{-nV}
corresponding to the classical Laguerre-type OP ensemble.

The relevance of these quantities is the following. The norming constants relate to the partition function via the identity (e.g., see
[
42
]
)

(2.11)

\mathsf{Z}_{n}(\mathsf{s})=n!\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}\gamma_{j}^{(n)}(\mathsf{s})^{-2}.

The ensemble (
2.1
) itself forms a determinantal point process: the joint distribution (
2.1
) may be written in the determinantal form

\det\left(\widehat{\mathsf{K}}_{n}(x_{i},x_{j}\mid\mathsf{s})\right)_{i,j=1}^{n}.

From this determinantal structure, statistics of the ensemble (
2.1
) may be computed using the theory of determinantal point processes
[
50
,
6
]
. In this regard, the multiplicative statistics
\mathsf{L}_{n}^{Q}(\mathsf{s})
from (
2.1
) can be computed in terms of a Fredholm determinant involving the kernel
\mathsf{K}_{n}(\cdot\mid\infty)
of the original Laguerre-type ensemble. However, such an expression is not appropriate for asymptotics, as one would still need to analyse a full Fredholm determinant. Our starting point will be instead a trace-type formula that allows to compute
\mathsf{L}_{n}^{Q}(\mathsf{s})
directly in terms of the diagonal of the deformed kernel
\mathsf{K}_{n}(\cdot\mid\mathsf{s})
. With
\mathsf{L}_{n}^{Q},\mathsf{K}_{n}
and
\omega_{n}
defined in (
2.5
), (
2.9
) and (
2.2
), respectively,
this formula is

(2.12)

\log\mathsf{L}_{n}^{Q}(\mathsf{s})=-\int_{\mathsf{s}}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathsf{K}_{n}(x,x\mid u)\partial_{u}\omega_{n}(x\mid u)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}u.

A variant of (
2.12
) appeared in
[
37
, Proposition 9.1]
, where it was proven in the particular context of OPs over the full real line. Other variants also appeared in
[
21
, End of Section 5.2]
and
[
14
, Theorem 1.10]
(see also
[
13
,
12
]
), which obtain their analogues exploring the Jacobi differentiation identity for Fredholm determinants of so-called IIKS integrable operators. The formula as it appears here is a particular case of
[
38
, Proposition 4.1]
, which provides a deformation formula valid for general OP ensembles (including discrete ones), deformations of weights beyond the case (
2.2
), and which relies only on the orthogonality of the polynomials
\mathsf{P}_{j}
used to construct the kernel
\mathsf{K}_{n}(\cdot\mid\mathsf{s})
.

Thanks to (
2.12
), the first step in understanding
\mathsf{L}_{n}^{Q}(\mathsf{s})
as
n\to\infty
relies in obtaining asymptotics for
\mathsf{K}_{n}(\cdot\mid\mathsf{s})
. It turns out that in the large
n
limit, the major contribution to the
x
-integration in (
2.12
) comes from a neighborhood of the origin, precisely where
\mathsf{K}_{n}
has a novel scaling limit. To describe this scaling limit, let us introduce the function

(2.13)

\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}(\zeta)=\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}(\zeta\mid\mathsf{s})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-(-1)^{m}\zeta^{m}}}.

In this definition, the index
\mathbf{\Phi}
is introduced just for notational convenience, to distinguish between a generic function
\sigma
which will be used later on.

Theorem 2.3
.

Fix
\alpha>-1
. For
(\zeta,\mathsf{s},\mathsf{x})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times(0,+\infty)
, the nonlocal differential equation

(2.14)

\partial^{2}_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi(\zeta\mid\mathsf{s},\mathsf{x})=\left(\zeta+\frac{4\alpha^{2}-1}{4\mathsf{x}^{2}}+\frac{2\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\pi}\int_{\mathsf{s}}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\Phi(\xi\mid u,\mathsf{x})\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi(\xi\mid u,\mathsf{x})\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}(\xi\mid u)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}u\right)\Phi(\zeta\mid\mathsf{s},\mathsf{x})

admits a solution
\Phi=\Phi(\zeta\mid\mathsf{s},\mathsf{x})
satisfying for each
\mathsf{x}>0
fixed,

\displaystyle\Phi(\zeta)=\left(1+\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-1})\right)\frac{\zeta^{-1/4}}{\sqrt{2}}\ee^{\mathsf{x}\zeta^{1/2}},\quad\zeta\to+\infty,

\displaystyle\Phi(\zeta)=\left(1+\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-1})\right)\sqrt{2}\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha/2}|\zeta|^{-1/4}\cos\left(\mathsf{x}|\zeta|^{1/2}-\frac{\pi}{4}-\frac{\pi\alpha}{2}\right),\quad\zeta\to-\infty.

Furthermore, as
\mathsf{x}\to 0^{+}
,

(2.15)

\Phi(\zeta\mid\mathsf{s},\mathsf{x})=\sqrt{\pi}\mathsf{x}^{1/2}I_{\alpha}(\mathsf{x}\zeta^{1/2})\left(1+\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{x}^{2+2\alpha}(1+\mathsf{x}|\zeta|^{1/2}))\right),

uniformly for
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
where
I_{\alpha}
is the modified Bessel function of the first kind with index
\alpha
, and for
\zeta<0
we must consider the
+
-boundary value of
I_{\alpha}
.

With the solution
\Phi
just discussed, we construct the kernel

(2.16)

\mathsf{K}_{\alpha}(u,v)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}\sqrt{\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}\left(-4u/\mathsf{x}^{2}\right)}\sqrt{\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}\left(-4v/\mathsf{x}^{2}\right)}}{2\pi(u-v)}\\
\times\left[\Phi\left(-\frac{4u}{\mathsf{x}^{2}}\right)(\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi)\left(-\frac{4v}{\mathsf{x}^{2}}\right)-\Phi\left(-\frac{4v}{\mathsf{x}^{2}}\right)(\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi)\left(-\frac{4u}{\mathsf{x}^{2}}\right)\right],

valid for
u\neq v
, and extended by continuity to
u=v
.
Observe that
\Phi=\Phi(\cdot\mid\mathsf{s},\mathsf{x})
, and therefore
\mathsf{K}_{\alpha}=\mathsf{K}_{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot\mid\mathsf{s},\mathsf{x})
.

For the next result, set

(2.17)

\mathsf{c}_{V}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\pi^{2}\kappa_{0}^{2},\qquad\text{and identify}\qquad\mathsf{x}=\left(\frac{4\mathsf{c}_{V}}{\mathsf{t}^{1/m}}\right)^{1/2},

where
\kappa_{0}>0
is as in (
2.4
) and we recall that
\mathsf{t}>0
is as in (
2.7
).

Theorem 2.4
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
and
2.2
and the identification (
2.17
), the estimate

\frac{1}{n^{2}\mathsf{c}_{V}}\widehat{\mathsf{K}}_{n}\left(\frac{u}{\mathsf{c}_{V}n^{2}},\frac{v}{\mathsf{c}_{V}n^{2}}\right)=\mathsf{K}_{\alpha}(u,v\mid\mathsf{s},\mathsf{x})+\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\mathsf{s}}n^{-\kappa}+n^{-2}),\quad n\to\infty,

holds uniformly for
u,v
in compact subsets of
(0,+\infty)
, and uniformly for
\mathsf{s}\geq\mathsf{s}_{0}
with
\mathsf{s}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
fixed, and for any fixed
\kappa\in(0,2)
.

The kernel
\widehat{\mathsf{K}}_{n}
is the correlation kernel of the deformed matrix model (
2.1
), and in the limit
\mathsf{s}\to+\infty
it degenerates to the regular (undeformed) matrix model with weight
x^{\alpha}\ee^{-nV(x)}
. It was shown in
[
56
]
that the latter converges to the Bessel kernel

(2.18)

\mathsf{J}_{\alpha}(u,v):=\frac{J_{\alpha}(\sqrt{u})\sqrt{v}J_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\sqrt{v})-\sqrt{u}J_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\sqrt{u})J_{\alpha}(\sqrt{v})}{2(u-v)},\quad u,v>0,

where
J_{\alpha}
is the Bessel function of the first kind of order
\alpha
. The uniformity of the bound (
2.4
) immediately yields the following corollary.

Corollary 2.5
.

The limit

\lim_{\mathsf{s}\to+\infty}\mathsf{K}_{\alpha}(u,v\mid\mathsf{s})=\mathsf{J}_{\alpha}(u,v)

holds pointwise for
u,v>0
, where
\mathsf{J}_{\alpha}
is the Bessel kernel of order
\alpha>-1
defined in (
2.18
).

When scaled around the origin, the smallest eigenvalues of the model (
2.1
) converge to the Bessel point process (in the weak sense), which is the determinantal point process defined by the kernel
\mathsf{J}_{\alpha}
. Corollary
2.5
is the kernel convergence reflecting this property.

Next, we turn to the asymptotics for
\mathsf{L}_{n}^{Q}
, for which we introduce

(2.19)

\mathsf{L}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(\mathsf{s},\mathsf{x})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\exp\left(-\int_{\mathsf{s}}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathsf{K}_{\alpha}(\zeta,\zeta\mid\mathsf{s}=u)(\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\log\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}})\left(-\frac{4\zeta}{\mathsf{x}^{2}}\mid\mathsf{s}=u\right)\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}u\right).

The meaning of the upper index
\mathrm{(Bes)}
in the definition of
\mathsf{L}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}
will be explained in a moment.

Theorem 2.6
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
and
2.2
and the identification (
2.17
), the estimate

\log\mathsf{L}_{n}^{Q}(\mathsf{s})=\log\mathsf{L}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(\mathsf{s},\mathsf{x})+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\ee^{-\mathsf{s}}}{n}\right),\quad n\to\infty,

holds uniformly for
\mathsf{s}\geq\mathsf{s}_{0}
with any fixed
\mathsf{s}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
.

As mentioned earlier, the kernel
\widehat{\mathsf{K}}_{n}
is the correlation kernel for the conditional thinning of the eigenvalues of (
2.1
). In the recent work
[
22
]
, Claeys and the second-named author give conditions on the symbol
\sigma_{n}
in (
2.5
) under which (1) the conditional thinning of a finite
n
random particle system converges to the conditional thinning of the large
n
limit of the particle system, and (2) multiplicative statistics of the finite
n
random particle system converge to the multiplicative statistics of their large
n
limit.

Modulo checking the technical conditions required to apply the results from
[
22
]
, regarding (1),
Theorem
2.4
establishes that the kernel for the conditional thinning process of the Bessel point process associated to the symbol
\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}(-4\zeta\mathsf{x}^{-2}\mid\mathsf{s},\mathsf{x})
is precisely the kernel
\mathsf{K}_{\alpha}
. Regarding (2), Theorem
2.6
is saying that
\mathsf{L}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(\mathsf{s})
coincides with the multiplicative statistics of the Bessel point process for the same symbol, which also explains why we used the upper index
\mathrm{(Bes)}
.

This way, we refer to
\mathsf{K}_{\alpha}
as the conditional thinning kernel of the Bessel point process, and
\mathsf{L}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}
as a multiplicative statistic of the Bessel point process.
Checking the requirements for the application of the results in
[
22
]
is a technical feat that we will not delve into.

With
\Phi
as in Theorem
2.3
, introduce

(2.20)

\mathsf{p}(\mathsf{s},\mathsf{x})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=-\frac{4\alpha^{2}-1}{8\mathsf{x}}+\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{2\pi}\int_{\mathsf{s}}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\Phi(\xi\mid\mathsf{s}=u,\mathsf{x})^{2}\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}(\xi\mid\mathsf{s}=u)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}u.

We may view (
2.14
) as a linear Schrödinger equation with potential
2\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{p}
, namely

\partial_{\mathsf{x}}^{2}\Phi(\zeta\mid\mathsf{s},\mathsf{x})=\left(\zeta+2\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{p}(\mathsf{s},\mathsf{x})\right)\Phi(\zeta\mid\mathsf{s},\mathsf{x}).

It turns out that we can relate the multiplicative statistic
\mathsf{L}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}
directly to the potential
\mathsf{p}
.

Theorem 2.7
.

Fix
\alpha>-1
. The estimate

(2.21)

\mathsf{p}(\mathsf{s},\mathsf{x})=-\frac{4\alpha^{2}-1}{8\mathsf{x}}+\frac{1}{2^{2\alpha+1}\Gamma(\alpha+1)^{2}}\mathsf{x}^{2\alpha+1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{u^{\alpha}\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-u^{m}}}{1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-u^{m}}}\mathrm{d}u+\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{x}^{2\alpha+3}),\quad\mathsf{x}\to 0^{+},

holds. In addition, the identity

(2.22)

\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\left(\log\mathsf{L}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(\mathsf{s},\mathsf{x})\right)=-\mathsf{p}(\mathsf{s},\mathsf{x})-\frac{4\alpha^{2}-1}{8\mathsf{x}}

is also true.

Using (
2.15
), it is straightforward to show that
\log\mathsf{L}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}
goes to
0
as
\mathsf{x}\to 0^{+}
. In particular, Theorem
2.7
then provides the representation

\log\mathsf{L}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(\mathsf{s},\mathsf{x})=-\int_{0}^{\mathsf{x}}\left(\mathsf{p}(\mathsf{s},u)+\frac{4\alpha^{2}-1}{8u}\right)\mathrm{d}u.

The celebrated
\beta=2
Tracy-Widom distribution had its name coined thanks to the work of Tracy and Widom
[
53
]
, who showed that the second log derivative of a multiplicative statistic of random matrices (a gap probability) at the soft edge could be expressed in terms of the Hastings-McLeod solution to the Painlevé II equation. The analogue representation for gap probabilities of the Bessel kernel in terms of the Painlevé V equation was obtained shortly afterwards by Tracy and Widom as well
[
52
]
. Several different multivariate extensions have been obtained in the past 20 years
[
54
,
1
,
57
,
8
,
3
,
7
]
.

Theorem
2.7
is the analogue representation, showing that the second log derivative of
\mathsf{L}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}
may be expressed in terms of
\mathsf{p}
which, in turn, is given in terms of the solution to the nonlocal nonlinear equation (
2.14
). In the case
m=1
, it is possible to obtain a PDE satisfied by
\mathsf{p}
directly.

Theorem 2.8
.

Suppose that
m=1
in (
2.3
). Then the function
\mathsf{p}
from (
2.20
) satisfies the PDE

\partial^{4}_{\mathsf{s}\mathsf{x}\mathsf{x}\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{p}-8\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{p}\left(\partial^{2}_{\mathsf{s}\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{p}+\frac{1}{2}\right)-4\partial^{2}_{\mathsf{x}\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{p}\left(\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{p}+\frac{\mathsf{x}}{2}\right)=0.

As said earlier, some of our results are in direct comparison with the recent work
[
46
]
by Ruzza. Therein, and as mentioned earlier, Ruzza considers a class of multiplicative statistics for the Bessel point process. His multiplicative statistics are coming from linear scalings of fixed functions which are not necessarily continuous, and our family of statistics
\mathsf{L}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}
reduces to a particular instance of his setup
1
1

1

With the identification of variables
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{s})=(x,-t)
, our function
\mathsf{p}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{s})
is comparable with the function
v(x,t)
in
[
46
]
through the relation

\mathsf{p}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{s})=-\mathrm{i}\left(v(x,t)+\frac{xt}{2}\right).

only when
m=1
. This way, Theorem
2.8
is a rediscovery of
[
46
, Theorem 1.1–(ii)]
, the case
m=1
of (
2.14
) with boundary condition (
2.15
) is equivalent to
[
46
, Theorem 1.1–(iii)]
, and the case
m=1
of (
2.21
) is the equivalent of
[
46
, Theorem 1.6]
.

Organization of the paper

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows.

Section
3
is devoted to the construction and analysis of a model RHP, which plays a central role in the asymptotic analysis of OPs carried out later. This model problem governs the hard edge statistics. We establish the solvability of this model problem and derive the nonlocal differential equations satisfied by its solution.

In Section
4
, we perform a detailed asymptotic analysis of the model RHP with respect to a large parameter (which corresponds to the system size
n
), and analyze its limiting behavior in relevant regimes.

Section
5
wraps up the asymptotic consequences from Section
4
, in particular establishing certain asymptotic properties of the correlation kernel, and related integrals that will later appear in the analysis of multiplicative statistics. We also prove main theorems on integrable equations in this section.

Finally, Sections
6
and
7
contain the asymptotic analysis of the orthogonal polynomials via the Deift-Zhou nonlinear steepest descent method, where the model problem and its properties established in the previous sections play a fundamental role.

About the notation

We use
D_{r}(z_{0})
to denote the disk on the complex plane centered at
z_{0}
with radius
r>0
, and
D_{r}=D_{r}(0)
for the particular case when
z_{0}=0
is the origin. In general, we use bold capital letters
\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{\Psi}
etc. to denote matrix-valued functions. The letters
\varepsilon,\delta,\eta
always denote positive constants that can be made arbitrarily small but are kept fixed, and we always emphasize when they may depend on external parameters. These small constants may have different values for different occurrences in the text. The set of strictly positive integers is
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, and we also use the notation
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\cup\{0\}
.

When we write that
x\to\infty
for some variable
x
, we mean that
x\to+\infty
when
x
is real, or
x\to\infty
along any direction of the complex plane in case
x
is allowed to assume values in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
as well. These two distinct cases will always be clear from the context and meaning of the variables involved.

We also use the following matrix notation. We denote by
\mathbf{I}
and
\mathbf{0}
the identity matrix and the null matrix, respectively, and by
\mathbf{E}_{ij}
the
2\times 2
matrix with
1
in the
(i,j)
-entry and
0
in the remaining entries. For convenience, we set

(2.23)

\mathbf{U}_{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathrm{i}\\
\mathrm{i}&1\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathrm{i}\mathbf{E}_{12}+\mathrm{i}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right),\quad\bm{\sigma}_{3}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}=\mathbf{E}_{11}-\mathbf{E}_{22}.

In the course of the Riemann-Hilbert analysis, we will use matrix norm notation. For a matrix-valued function
M:U\subset\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
, we denote

|M(z)|\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\max_{i,j=1,2}|M_{ij}(z)|,

where
M_{ij}
stands for the
(i,j)
-th entry of
M
.
For
p\in[1,\infty]
and a curve
\Gamma\subset U
, we also use the corresponding
L^{p}(\Gamma)=L^{p}(\Gamma,|\mathrm{d}s|)
norm with respect to the arc length measure
|\mathrm{d}s|
,

\|M\|_{L^{p}(\Gamma)}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\max_{i,j=1,2}\|M_{ij}\|_{L^{p}(\Gamma)}.

For a matrix-valued function
M
depending on a variable
x\in\mathbb{R}
, it is convenient to introduce the notation

(2.24)

\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}M(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=M(x)^{-1}M^{\prime}(x).

Under a change of variables
x\mapsto\zeta=\zeta(x)
, this operator transforms as

(2.25)

\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}(M(\zeta(x))=\zeta^{\prime}(x)M(\zeta(x))^{-1}\frac{\mathrm{d}M}{\mathrm{d}\zeta}(\zeta(x))=\zeta^{\prime}\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}M(\zeta).

3.
The model RHP

As mentioned earlier, the main technical tool we utilize is the RHP formulation of OPs and associated quantities. When we perform the asymptotic analysis of the OPs via the RHP method, at one of the core steps we need to construct an approximation to them near the origin, the so-called model RHP for the local parametrix. For usual Laguerre-type OPs, such approximation is constructed using Bessel functions as originally developed in
[
40
]
; see
[
56
]
for details. Due to the nontrivial scaling of
\sigma_{n}
near the origin, we need to use a different model problem, which we introduce and study in this section.

This section is structured as follows. In Section
3.1
we introduce the model problem itself. In Section
3.2
we establish the solvability of the model RHP, by means of vanishing-lemma-like arguments. In Section
3.3
, we connect the model problem with integrable systems; the content of the latter section ultimately leads to the differential equations from our main results.

3.1.
The model problem

Fix
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, which will have the same meaning as in (
2.7
), and set

(3.1)

\theta_{m}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\pi\left(1-\frac{1}{3m}\right),\quad\Gamma_{\pm}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(\ee^{\pm\mathrm{i}\theta_{m}}\infty,0],\quad\Gamma_{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(-\infty,0],\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Gamma_{+}\cup\Gamma_{0}\cup\Gamma_{-},

where the orientations of the arcs
\Gamma_{\pm},\Gamma_{0}
are taken from
\infty
to the origin. Also, introduce the sectors

(3.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{\zeta\in\mathbb{C}\mid-\theta_{m}<\arg\zeta<\theta_{m}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{+}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{\zeta\in\mathbb{C}\mid\theta_{m}<\arg\zeta<\pi\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{-}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{\zeta\in\mathbb{C}\mid-\pi<\arg\zeta<-\theta_{m}\},

with principal value of the argument, that is,
\arg\zeta\in(-\pi,\pi)
. We refer to Figure
1
for an illustration of these arcs and sectors.

Figure 1
.
The contours
\Gamma_{\pm}
,
\Gamma_{0}
, and the sectors
\mathcal{S}_{\pm}
,
\mathcal{S}_{0}
.

Observe that

\partial\mathcal{S}_{0}=\Gamma_{+}\cup\Gamma_{-},\quad\partial\mathcal{S}_{+}=\Gamma_{+}\cup\Gamma_{0},\quad\partial\mathcal{S}_{-}=\Gamma_{-}\cup\Gamma_{0}.

The value
\theta_{m}
is chosen so that

(3.3)

\frac{1}{2}|\zeta|^{m}\leq(-1)^{m}\re(\zeta^{m})\leq|\zeta|^{m}\quad\text{for}\quad\zeta\in\mathcal{S}_{+}\cup\mathcal{S}_{-},

with the upper bound being attained only along
\partial\mathcal{S}_{+}\cap\partial\mathcal{S}_{-}=\Gamma_{0}
, and the lower bound being attained only along
(\partial\mathcal{S}_{+}\cup\partial\mathcal{S}_{-})\setminus\Gamma_{0}=\Gamma_{+}\cup\Gamma_{-}
.

Fix
\alpha>-1
(which has the same meaning as in (
2.2
)) and a function
\mathsf{h}:\Gamma\to\mathbb{C}
. We are interested in the following RHP.

RHP 3.1
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{\Psi}:\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
with the following properties.

(i)

\mathbf{\Psi}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma
.

(ii)

The entries of
\mathbf{\Psi}_{\pm}
are continuous except possibly at the origin, and for
\zeta\in\Gamma\setminus\{0\}
they satisfy the jump relation

\mathbf{\Psi}_{+}(\zeta)=\mathbf{\Psi}_{-}(\zeta)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{\Psi}}(\zeta),

with jump matrix

(3.4)

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{\Psi}}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}+(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}(\zeta)})\ee^{\pm\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}\mathbf{E}_{21},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{\pm},\\
\frac{1}{1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}(\zeta)}}\mathbf{E}_{12}-(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}(\zeta)})\mathbf{E}_{21},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{0}.\end{cases}

(iii)

For some matrix-valued function
\mathbf{\Psi}_{1}
which is independent of
\zeta
, the following expansion is valid,

(3.5)

\mathbf{\Psi}(\zeta)=\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\mathbf{\Psi}_{1}}{\zeta}+\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-2})\right)\zeta^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\ee^{2\zeta^{1/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}},\quad\zeta\to\infty,

with the principal branches of the fractional powers, and where
\mathbf{U}_{0}
and
\bm{\sigma}_{3}
are defined in (
2.23
).

(iv)

The entries of
\mathbf{\Psi}_{\pm}
belong to
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Gamma,|\mathrm{d}s|)
.

Condition (ii) asks for continuity of the boundary values
\mathbf{\Psi}_{\pm}
except possibly at the origin, so Condition (iv) is an additional requirement only about the behavior of
\mathbf{\Psi}
near the origin.

In the statement of RHP
3.1
above, we have used the function
\mathsf{h}
, but in fact the jump may be written simply in terms of the function
\sigma
given by

(3.6)

\sigma:\Gamma\to\mathbb{C},\quad\sigma(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}(\zeta)}},

namely as

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{\Psi}}(\zeta)=\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}+\frac{\ee^{\pm\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\sigma(\zeta)}\mathbf{E}_{21},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{\pm},\\
\sigma(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{12}-\frac{1}{\sigma(\zeta)}\mathbf{E}_{21},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{0}.\end{cases}

Historically, factors of the form
(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}})^{-1}
have appeared in the so-called finite temperature deformations of random matrix models, and partly for that reason oftentimes it is convenient to state conditions/results in terms of
\mathsf{h}
. However, in what follows we may use either
\sigma
or
\mathsf{h}
, depending on the convenience of the situation.

Obviously,
\mathbf{\Psi}
depends on
\mathsf{h},\alpha
, and when we need to make this dependence explicit we write
\mathbf{\Psi}=\mathbf{\Psi}(\cdot\mid\mathsf{h}),\mathbf{\Psi}(\cdot\mid\alpha),\mathbf{\Psi}(\cdot\mid\mathsf{h},\alpha)
, etc. For the RHP above to be well-posed, we should impose conditions on
\mathsf{h}
. Global conditions on
\mathsf{h}
will be imposed in a moment, but it is convenient to write the condition (iv) in a more explicit manner when
\mathsf{h}
is analytic at the origin, as given by the next result. To proceed, we introduce

(3.7)

\mathsf{a}(\zeta)=\mathsf{a}_{\alpha}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2\mathrm{i}\sin(\alpha\pi)},&\alpha\notin\mathbb{Z},\alpha>-1,\\
\frac{\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\log\zeta,&\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\end{cases}

for
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0]
. It is easily seen that
\mathsf{a}
satisfies the jump condition

(3.8)

\mathsf{a}_{+}(\zeta)\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}-\mathsf{a}_{-}(\zeta)\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}=1,\qquad\zeta<0,

for any
\alpha>-1
. This function will be used several times throughout the text.

Proposition 3.2
.

Suppose that
\mathsf{h}
is analytic in a neighborhood of the origin and
1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}}
never vanishes near the origin. Then Condition (iv) in RHP
3.1
implies that for some
\varepsilon>0
, the identities

(3.9)

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Psi}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}(\zeta)\zeta^{\alpha\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}(\mathbf{I}+\mathsf{a}(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{12})\sigma(\zeta)^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}(\mathbf{I}-\sigma(\zeta)^{-1}(\chi_{\mathcal{S}_{+}}(\zeta)\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}-\chi_{\mathcal{S}_{-}}(\zeta)\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha})\mathbf{E}_{21})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}(\zeta)\zeta^{\alpha\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}(\mathbf{I}+\mathsf{a}(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{12})(\mathbf{I}-(\chi_{\mathcal{S}_{+}}(\zeta)\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}-\chi_{\mathcal{S}_{-}}(\zeta)\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha})\mathbf{E}_{21})\sigma(\zeta)^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}

hold for
\zeta\in D_{\varepsilon}
, where
\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}(\zeta)
is analytic on
D_{\varepsilon}
, all the roots are principal branches, and
\chi_{\mathcal{S}_{\pm}}
is the characteristic function of the set
\mathcal{S}_{\pm}
.

Proof.

The second equality in (
3.9
) follows from a simple algebraic manipulation, which we now use without further justification.

We use the first identity in (
3.9
) as a defining relation for
\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}
and prove that it is an analytic function near the origin. Note that the factors
\zeta^{\alpha/2},\sigma^{-1/2}
and
\mathsf{a}
are analytic and non-vanishing on a set of the form
D_{\varepsilon}\setminus(-\varepsilon,0]
, from which it follows that
\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}
is piecewise analytic, with jumps along
\Gamma
. A direct calculation, also using the analyticity of
\zeta^{\alpha/2},\sigma^{-1/2},\mathsf{a}
away from the negative axis, shows that
\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}
is analytic across
\Gamma_{+}
and
\Gamma_{-}
as well. For
\zeta\in\Gamma_{0}\setminus\{0\}
, we compute

\mathbf{\Psi}_{0,-}(\zeta)^{-1}\mathbf{\Psi}_{0,+}(\zeta)=\mathbf{I}+|\zeta|^{\alpha}\left(1-\mathsf{a}_{+}(\xi)\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}+\mathsf{a}_{-}(\zeta)\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}\right)\mathbf{E}_{12}.

In virtue of (
3.8
), the right-hand side above is equal to
\mathbf{I}
, and we conclude that
\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}
is analytic across
\Gamma_{0}
as well.

The arguments above show that
\zeta=0
is an isolated singularity of
\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}
. Because we are assuming that the singularities of the entries of
\mathbf{\Psi}
at the origin are square-integrable and
\alpha>-1
, we see that the entries of
\mathbf{\Psi}_{0,\pm}
belong to
L^{1}
, implying that
\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}
has a removable singularity at
\zeta=0
.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

The previous proposition requires that
\sigma^{-1}=1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}}
is analytic and non-vanishing near the origin, but not necessarily
\mathsf{h}
itself. It is thus immediate to see that its conclusion also holds for the choice
\mathsf{h}=+\infty
, which leads to
1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}}\equiv 1
; for this choice the RHP for
\mathbf{\Psi}
reduces to a RHP for Bessel functions (see Appendix
A
below), which is known and will be used later.

\triangleright

For the remainder of this section we are interested in two independent aspects. First of all, under some additional conditions on
\mathsf{h}
we will prove that the model problem is solvable. Second, for certain particular choices of
\mathsf{h}
, we explain how our model problem is connected to a family of integrable systems.

3.2.
Solvability of the model problem

Our goal in this section is to prove that under some conditions on
\mathsf{h}
, the RHP
3.1
for
\mathbf{\Psi}
always admits a solution. Recall the contours
\Gamma_{\pm},\Gamma_{0}
and the sectors
\mathcal{S}_{0},\mathcal{S}_{\pm}
defined in (
3.1
) and (
3.2
), respectively, we
assume the following.

Assumption 3.4
.

The function
\mathsf{h}
in (
3.4
) satisfies the following conditions.

(i)

\mathsf{h}:G\to\mathbb{C}
is well-defined and analytic in some open set
G
that contains the closure of the union of sectors
{\mathcal{S}}_{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{-}
.

(ii)

\mathsf{h}
is real-valued along
(-\infty,0)
.

(iii)

The associated function
\sigma
from (
3.6
) extends to
G
with a bounded control. More precisely,

\sigma:G\to\mathbb{C},\quad\sigma(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}(\zeta)}},

remains bounded as
\zeta\to\infty
along
G
, and furthermore

\sigma(\zeta)=1+\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-2}),

as
\zeta\to\infty
in a neighborhood of
(-\infty,0)
.

The final goal of this section is to prove the following result.

Theorem 3.5
.

Under Assumption
3.4
, RHP
3.1
for
\mathbf{\Psi}
admits a unique solution.

The uniqueness claim in Theorem
3.5
follows in a simple standard way in RHP theory
[
31
]
. We will establish the existence of the solution
\mathbf{\Psi}
by means of the technique of a vanishing lemma. Following the general scheme laid out in
[
29
,
35
,
59
]
, a key step in the argument is to show that the “homogeneous” version of the RHP has only the trivial solution. Over here, the relevant homogeneous version of the RHP
3.1
is the following.

RHP 3.6
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{\Psi}^{\rm H}:\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
with the following properties.

(i)

\mathbf{\Psi}^{\rm H}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma
.

(ii)

The entries of
\mathbf{\Psi}_{\pm}^{\rm H}
are continuous except possibly at
\zeta=0
, and for
\zeta\in\Gamma\setminus\{0\}
they satisfy the jump relation

\mathbf{\Psi}^{\rm H}_{+}(\zeta)=\mathbf{\Psi}^{\rm H}_{-}(\zeta)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{\Psi}}(\zeta),

with the same jump matrix
\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{\Psi}}
as in (
3.4
).

(iii)

As
\zeta\to\infty
,

(3.10)

\mathbf{\Psi}^{\rm H}(\zeta)=\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-1})\zeta^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\ee^{2\zeta^{1/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}.

(iv)

As
\zeta\to 0
,
it behaves as

(3.11)

\mathbf{\Psi}^{\rm H}(\zeta)=\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{\rm H}(\zeta)\zeta^{\alpha\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}(\mathbf{I}+\mathsf{a}(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{12})(\mathbf{I}-(\chi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{+}}(\zeta)\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}-\chi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{-}}(\zeta)\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha})\mathbf{E}_{21})\sigma(\zeta)^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2},

where
\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{\rm H}
is analytic in a neighborhood of the origin,
\mathsf{a}
and
\sigma
are defined in (
3.7
) and (
3.6
), respectively.

As mentioned, from standard RHP theory it follows that Theorem
3.5
is proved once we establish the next lemma, which is usually referred to as a vanishing lemma.

Lemma 3.7
.

Under Assumption
3.4
, the unique solution to RHP
3.6
is the trivial solution
\mathbf{\Psi}^{\rm H}\equiv 0
.

This way, for the remainder of this section our work will be focused towards proving Lemma
3.7
, which will be established following a standard route of making certain transformations of the homogeneous RHP, and then applying the theory of analytic functions to prove that the entries of a transformed version of
\mathbf{\Psi}^{\rm H}
are all identically zero.

As a first step, we explore that the function
\mathsf{h}
is analytic on a neighborhood of
{\mathcal{S}}_{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{-}
, and transform

\mathbf{\Phi}^{\rm H}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{\Psi}^{\rm H}(\zeta)\left(\mathbf{I}+\sigma(\zeta)^{-1}\left(\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}\chi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{+}}(\zeta)-\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}\chi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{-}}(\zeta)\right)\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\ee^{-2\zeta^{1/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}.

This transformation has the effect of removing the jumps outside the real axis, and also to remove the exponential behavior from the asymptotics at
\infty
. More precisely, a direct calculation shows that the function
\mathbf{\Phi}^{\rm H}
solves the following RHP.

RHP 3.8
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{\Phi}^{\rm H}:\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0]\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
with the following properties.

(i)

\mathbf{\Phi}^{\rm H}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0]
.

(ii)

The entries of
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\pm}^{\rm H}
are continuous except possibly at the origin, and for
\zeta\in(-\infty,0)
they satisfy the jump relation

(3.12)

\mathbf{\Phi}^{\rm H}_{+}(\zeta)=\mathbf{\Phi}^{\rm H}_{-}(\zeta)\left(\ee^{(\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha-4\mathrm{i}|\zeta|^{1/2})\bm{\sigma}_{3}}+\sigma(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{12}\right).

(iii)

As
\zeta\to\infty
,

(3.13)

\mathbf{\Phi}^{\rm H}(\zeta)=\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-1})\zeta^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\mathcal{O}(1)=\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-3/4}).

(iv)

As
\zeta\to 0
, it behaves as

\mathbf{\Phi}^{\rm H}(\zeta)=\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{\rm H}(\zeta)\zeta^{\alpha\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}(\mathbf{I}+\mathsf{a}(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{12})\mathcal{O}(1),

where
\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{\rm H}
is analytic in a neighborhood of the origin as given in (
3.11
).

Next, set

\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{\Phi}^{\rm H}(\zeta)\begin{cases}\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},&\im\zeta>0,\\
\mathbf{I},&\im\zeta<0.\end{cases}

With this transformation, we move the diagonal entries in (
3.12
) to off-diagonal entries, and the matrix
\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}
is a solution to the following RHP.

RHP 3.9
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}:\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
with the following properties.

(i)

\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
.

(ii)

The entries of
\mathbf{X}_{\pm}^{\rm H}
are continuous except possibly at the origin, and for
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}
they satisfy the jump relation

(3.14)

\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}_{+}(\zeta)=\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}_{-}(\zeta)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}}(\zeta),

with jump matrix

(3.15)

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}}(\zeta)=\begin{cases}\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},&\zeta>0,\\
\begin{pmatrix}\sigma(\zeta)&-\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha-4\mathrm{i}|\zeta|^{1/2}}\\
\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha+4\mathrm{i}|\zeta|^{1/2}}&0\end{pmatrix},&\zeta<0.\end{cases}

(iii)

As
\zeta\to\infty
,

(3.16)

\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}(\zeta)=\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-3/4}).

(iv)

As
\zeta\to 0
, it behaves as

\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}(\zeta)=\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{\rm H}(\zeta)\zeta^{\alpha\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}(\mathbf{I}+\mathsf{a}(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{12})\mathcal{O}(1),

where
\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{\rm H}
is analytic in a neighborhood of the origin as given in (
3.11
).

We are now ready to prove Lemma
3.7
and thus conclude Theorem
3.5
.

Proof of Lemma
3.7
.

Denote by
M^{*}
the adjoint (complex transpose) of a matrix
M
. The jump matrix
\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}}
enjoys the symmetry properties

(3.17)

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}}(\zeta)+\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}}(\zeta)^{*}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{0},&\zeta>0,\\
2\sigma(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{11},&\zeta<0.\end{cases}

To explore these symmetries, let us set

\mathbf{H}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}(\zeta)\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}(\overline{\zeta})^{*},\quad\zeta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}.

Then
\mathbf{H}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
and has the behavior

\mathbf{H}(\zeta)=\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-3/2}),\quad\zeta\to\infty.

Using (
3.7
) and the definitions of
\mathbf{H}
and
\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}
, it also follows that

\mathbf{H}(\zeta)=\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{\alpha}(1+\mathsf{c}_{\alpha}\log\zeta)),\quad\zeta\to 0,

for some constant
\mathsf{c}_{\alpha}
depending on
\alpha
with
\mathsf{c}_{\alpha}=0
if
\alpha\notin\mathbb{Z}
. In particular, this transformation regularizes the behavior at the origin and at
\infty
, to an integrable behavior. Using Cauchy’s Theorem, we then obtain

\mathbf{0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbf{H}_{+}(\zeta)\,\mathrm{d}\zeta=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbf{X}_{-}^{\rm H}(\zeta)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}}(\zeta)(\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}(\overline{\zeta})^{*})_{+}\,\mathrm{d}\zeta=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbf{X}_{-}^{\rm H}(\zeta)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}}(\zeta)\mathbf{X}_{-}^{\rm H}(\zeta)^{*}\,\mathrm{d}\zeta.

Adding the integral on the right-hand side with its adjoint and then using (
3.17
) we obtain the identity

\int_{-\infty}^{0}\mathbf{X}_{-}^{\rm H}(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{11}\mathbf{X}_{-}^{\rm H}(\zeta)^{*}\sigma(\zeta)\,\mathrm{d}\zeta=\mathbf{0}.

Writing entry-wise, and using that
\sigma>0
along the real axis, we have

\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}_{11,-}(\zeta)=\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}_{21,-}(\zeta)=0,\quad\zeta<0.

With the aid of the jump relation (
3.14
) and (
3.15
) satisfied by
\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}
, we further conclude that

\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}_{12,+}(\zeta)=\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}_{22,+}(\zeta)=0,\quad\zeta<0.

Moreover, with the principal branch of
\zeta^{1/2}
,
the jump relation of
\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}
also implies that for
j=1,2
, the function

\zeta\mapsto\begin{cases}\mathbf{X}_{j2}^{\rm H}(\zeta),&\zeta\in\mathcal{S}_{+},\\
-\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha+4\zeta^{1/2}}\mathbf{X}_{j1}^{\rm H}(\zeta),&\zeta\in\mathcal{S}_{-},\end{cases}

is analytic on a neighborhood of the interval
(-\infty,0)
, and by the identities we just obtained it vanishes on this interval. Hence, these functions are identically zero on
S_{\pm}
, and by analytic continuation we conclude that

(3.18)

\mathbf{X}_{11}^{\rm H}(\zeta)=\mathbf{X}_{21}^{\rm H}(\zeta)=0,\,\im\zeta<0,\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{X}_{12}^{\rm H}(\zeta)=\mathbf{X}_{22}^{\rm H}(\zeta)=0,\,\im\zeta>0.

As a final step, we now improve these identities to the vanishing of
\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}
in the whole complex plane. For that, we account for (
3.18
) and write the jump condition (
3.14
) and (
3.15
) for
\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}_{j1,+}(\zeta)=\left(\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}_{j2}(\zeta)\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha-4\zeta^{1/2}}\right)_{-},\quad

\displaystyle\zeta<0,

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}_{j1,+}(\zeta)=\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}_{j2,-}(\zeta),

\displaystyle\zeta>0,

where in the first line we use the principal branch of the square-root and
j=1,2
. Such jump conditions motivate us to define the scalar functions

(3.19)

\mathsf{g}_{j}^{\rm H}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{X}_{j1}^{\rm H}(\zeta),&\im\zeta>0,\\
\mathbf{X}_{j2}^{\rm H}(\zeta),&\im\zeta<0.\end{cases}

The properties of
\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}
we just discussed then imply that
\mathsf{g}_{j}^{\rm H}
is a solution to the following scalar RHP.

RHP 3.10
.

For
j=1,2
, the scalar function
\mathsf{g}_{j}^{\rm H}
defined in (
3.19
) satisfies the following conditions.

(i)

\mathsf{g}_{j}^{\rm H}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0]
.

(ii)

For
\zeta<0
, it satisfies the jump relation

(3.20)

\mathsf{g}_{j,+}^{\rm H}(\zeta)=\mathsf{g}_{j,-}^{\rm H}(\zeta)\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha+4\mathrm{i}|\zeta|^{1/2}}.

(iii)

As
\zeta\to\infty
,

\mathsf{g}_{j}^{\rm H}(\zeta)=\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-3/4}).

(iv)

As
\zeta\to 0
,

\mathsf{g}_{1}^{\rm H}(\zeta)=\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{\alpha/2}(1+\mathsf{c}_{\alpha}\log\zeta)),\quad\mathsf{g}_{2}^{\rm H}(\zeta)=\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-\alpha/2}(1+\mathsf{c}_{\alpha}\log\zeta)),

where
\mathsf{c}_{\alpha}\neq 0
only for
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

Let us now redefine

\widehat{\mathsf{g}}_{j}^{\rm H}(\zeta)=\mathsf{g}_{j}^{\rm H}(\zeta^{2}),\quad\re\zeta>0.

Then
\widehat{\mathsf{g}}_{j}^{\rm H}
is analytic on
\re\zeta>0
and it admits an analytic continuation to a sector containing the imaginary axis. In fact, the jump condition (
3.20
) for
\mathsf{g}_{j}^{\rm H}
along
(-\infty,0)
implies that for any
\theta\in(0,\pi/2)
, the function

\widehat{\mathsf{g}}_{j}^{\rm H}(\zeta)=\begin{cases}\mathsf{g}_{j}^{\rm H}(\zeta^{2}),&\re\zeta>0,\\
\mathsf{g}_{j}^{\rm H}(\zeta^{2})\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha+4\zeta},&\frac{\pi}{2}<\arg\zeta<\frac{\pi}{2}+\theta,\\
\mathsf{g}_{j}^{\rm H}(\zeta^{2})\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha+4\zeta},&-\frac{\pi}{2}-\theta<\arg\zeta<-\frac{\pi}{2},\end{cases}

is analytic across
\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, and satisfies

\widehat{\mathsf{g}}_{j}^{\rm H}(\zeta)=\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-3/2}),\;\zeta\to\infty\text{ with }-\frac{\pi}{2}-\theta<\arg\zeta<\frac{\pi}{2}+\theta,

as well as

\widehat{\mathsf{g}}_{1}^{\rm H}(\zeta)=\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{\alpha}(1+\mathsf{c}_{\alpha}\log\zeta)),\quad\widehat{\mathsf{g}}_{2}^{\rm H}(\zeta)=\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-\alpha}(1+\mathsf{c}_{\alpha}\log\zeta)),\quad\zeta\to 0.

Finally, let us further introduce

\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{j}^{\rm H}(\zeta)=\left(\frac{\zeta}{1+\zeta}\right)^{|\alpha|+1}\widehat{\mathsf{g}}_{j}^{\rm H}(\zeta).

It follows that
\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{j}^{\rm H}
is analytic on
-\frac{\pi}{2}-\theta<\arg\zeta<\frac{\pi}{2}+\theta
, it goes to
0
as
\zeta\to\infty
along the same sector, and it is also bounded as
\zeta\to 0
. Applying Phragmén-Lindelöf principle
[
49
, Section 5.1, in particular Exercise 5.1.2]
, it follows that
\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{j}^{\rm H}\equiv 0
.

Tracing back the transformations, this shows that the entries of
\mathbf{X}^{\rm H}
are all identically zero, as we wanted.
∎

3.3.
Differential equations for the model problem with some particular data

Consider the model problem with the particular choice

(3.21)

\mathsf{h}(\zeta)=\mathsf{h}_{\infty}(\zeta\mid\mathsf{s},\mathsf{u})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathsf{s}+\mathsf{u}(-1)^{m}\zeta^{m},\quad\mathsf{s}\in\mathbb{R},\quad\mathsf{u}>0.

The index
\infty
is used because later this choice
\mathsf{h}_{\infty}
will appear as a limiting data.

Let

(3.22)

\mathbf{\Psi}_{\infty}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{\Psi}(\zeta\mid\mathsf{h}=\mathsf{h}_{\infty},\alpha)

be the associated solution to the RHP
3.1
. Thanks to Theorem
3.5
, the solution
\mathbf{\Psi}_{\infty}
exists uniquely. The goal of this subsection is to connect certain quantities associated to
\mathbf{\Psi}_{\infty}
to integrable systems.
Recall that
\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}
was introduced in (
2.13
). It may be alternatively expressed as

(3.23)

\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}(\zeta)=\frac{1}{1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}_{\infty}(\zeta/\mathsf{u}^{1/m})}}.

For the following calculations, it turns out to be convenient to collapse the jumps outside the real axis down to the negative axis. This process is performed through the modification of
\mathbf{\Psi}_{\infty}
given by

(3.24)

{\mathbf{\Phi}}_{\infty}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathsf{u}^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/(4m)}\mathbf{\Psi}_{\infty}(\zeta/\mathsf{u}^{1/m})\left(\mathbf{I}+\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}(\zeta)^{-1}\left(\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}\chi_{\mathcal{S}_{+}}(\zeta)-\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}\chi_{\mathcal{S}_{-}}(\zeta)\right)\mathbf{E}_{21}\right),\quad\zeta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma,

where we recall that the sectors
\mathcal{S}_{\pm}
are displayed in Figure
1
. It then follows from RHP
3.1
for
\mathbf{\Psi}_{\infty}
and (
3.22
) that
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}
satisfies the following RHP.

RHP 3.11
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}:\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0]\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
with the following properties.

(i)

\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0]
.

(ii)

The entries of
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty,\pm}
are continuous along
(-\infty,0)
and they are related by

(3.25)

\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty,+}(\zeta)=\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty,-}(\zeta)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}}(\zeta),\quad\textrm{with}\quad\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha\bm{\sigma}_{3}}+\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{12}.

(iii)

There exists a matrix
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty,1}
, independent of
\zeta
, such that as
\zeta\to\infty
,

(3.26)

\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}(\zeta)=\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty,1}}{\zeta}+\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-2})\right)\zeta^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\ee^{\mathsf{x}\zeta^{1/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\left(\mathbf{I}+(\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}\chi_{\mathcal{S}_{+}}(\zeta)-\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}\chi_{\mathcal{S}_{-}}(\zeta))\mathbf{E}_{21}\right),

where we have set

(3.27)

\mathsf{x}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=2\mathsf{u}^{-\frac{1}{2m}}>0.

(iv)

There exists a matrix-valued function
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty,0}
which is analytic near
\zeta=0
for which

(3.28)

\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}(\zeta)=\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty,0}(\zeta)\zeta^{\alpha\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathsf{a}(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{12}\right)\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}(\zeta)^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2},\quad\zeta\to 0,

where
\mathsf{a}
is as in (
3.7
).

For later use, we record that the coefficient
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty,1}
is related with the coefficient
\mathbf{\Psi}_{1}=\mathbf{\Psi}_{\infty,1}(\mathsf{h}=\mathsf{h}_{\infty})
appearing in (
3.5
) through the identity

(3.29)

\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty,1}=\mathsf{u}^{1/m}\mathsf{u}^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/(4m)}\mathbf{\Psi}_{\infty,1}\mathsf{u}^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/(4m)}=\frac{4}{\mathsf{x}^{2}}2^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\mathsf{x}^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\mathbf{\Psi}_{\infty,1}\mathsf{x}^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}2^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}.

There are two major reasons for the change
\mathbf{\Psi}_{\infty}\mapsto\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}
. First, with the introduction of the characteristic functions in the right-most side of (
3.24
) the matrix
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}
has jumps only along
(-\infty,0)
. This structure will be useful later when deriving the mentioned nonlocal equation. Second, as a consequence of the change of variables
\zeta\mapsto\zeta/\mathsf{u}^{1/m}
in (
3.24
), we obtain that the jump matrix
\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}}
for
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}
is independent of the external parameter
\mathsf{x}
.

As a consequence of the latter property, we obtain a differential equation for
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}
in the variable
\mathsf{x}
. The argument is standard and goes as follows. Both
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}
and its derivative
\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}
have the same jump relations, and therefore the matrix
\mathbf{A}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}(\zeta)^{-1}
is analytic across
(-\infty,0)
. In other words,
\mathbf{A}
has an isolated singularity at
\zeta=0
, and it is analytic elsewhere in
\mathbb{C}
. From the explicit behavior of
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}
at
\zeta=0
in (
3.28
) we see that the singularity of
\mathbf{A}
at
\zeta=0
is removable, that is, all the entries of
\mathbf{A}
are entire functions. From the behavior of
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}
at
\zeta=\infty
in (
3.26
) we obtain an explicit expression for
\mathbf{A}
in terms of
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty,1}
. Writing

(3.30)

\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty,1}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{q}&-\mathrm{i}\mathsf{p}\\
\mathrm{i}\mathsf{r}&-\mathsf{q}\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{with}\quad\mathsf{q}=\mathsf{q}(\mathsf{s},\mathsf{x}),\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{r}(\mathsf{s},\mathsf{x})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{p}=\mathsf{p}(\mathsf{s},\mathsf{x})=\frac{2\mathrm{i}}{\mathsf{x}}(\mathbf{\Psi}_{\infty,1})_{12},

we obtain

(3.31)

\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}(\zeta)=\mathbf{A}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}(\zeta),\quad\text{with}\quad\mathbf{A}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{p}&-\mathrm{i}\\
\mathrm{i}(\zeta-2\mathsf{q})&-\mathsf{p}\end{pmatrix}.

Moreover, the
1/\zeta
term in the large
\zeta
-expansion of
\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}(\zeta)^{-1}

must vanish, and the
(1,2)
-entry therein gives us

(3.32)

\mathsf{p}^{2}=2\mathsf{q}+\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{p}.

Although the matrix
\mathbf{A}
is rather simple-looking, it is convenient to transform
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}
a little further, in order to obtain an ODE with a matrix coefficient with vanishing diagonal. To that end, we transform

(3.33)

\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(\mathbf{I}+\mathrm{i}\mathsf{p}\mathbf{E}_{21})\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}(\zeta).

A direct calculation using (
3.31
) and (
3.32
) then shows that
\mathbf{\Upsilon}
satisfies the ODE

(3.34)

\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)=\mathbf{B}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta),\qquad\mathbf{B}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{B}_{1}\zeta+\mathbf{B}_{0},\quad\mathbf{B}_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathrm{i}\mathbf{E}_{21},\quad\mathbf{B}_{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=2\mathrm{i}\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{p}\mathbf{E}_{21}-\mathrm{i}\mathbf{E}_{12}.

This new ODE implies in particular the relation

(3.35)

\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{2k}(\zeta)=\mathrm{i}\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{1k}(\zeta),\quad k=1,2.

Furthermore, taking one more
\partial_{\mathsf{x}}
-derivative in (
3.34
), looking entrywise, and setting

(3.36)

\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{1k}(\zeta)=\Phi_{k}(\zeta),\quad\Phi\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Phi_{1}=(\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty})_{11},

we obtain

(3.37)

\partial^{2}_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi_{k}(\zeta)=\left(\zeta+2\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{p}\right)\Phi_{k}(\zeta),\quad k=1,2.

The function
\Phi
plays a major role in our results. For all the calculations that follows, we understand

\Phi(\zeta)=\Phi_{+}(\zeta)\quad\text{for }\zeta<0,

having in mind also that by the jump condition (
3.25
),

(3.38)

\Phi(\zeta)=\Phi_{+}(\zeta)=\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}\Phi_{-}(\zeta),\quad\zeta<0.

Observe that the very definition of
\Phi
in (
3.36
) in terms of
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}
, when combined with (
3.26
), yields the asymptotic behaviors

(3.39)

\Phi(\zeta)=\left(1+\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-1})\right)\frac{\zeta^{-1/4}}{\sqrt{2}}\ee^{\mathsf{x}\zeta^{1/2}},\quad\zeta\to+\infty,

and

(3.40)

\Phi(\zeta)=\left(1+\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-1})\right)\sqrt{2}\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha/2+\pi\mathrm{i}/4}\zeta_{+}^{-1/4}\cos\left(\mathsf{x}|\zeta|^{1/2}-\frac{\pi}{4}-\frac{\pi\alpha}{2}\right),\quad\zeta\to-\infty.

In summary, equation (
3.34
) is a first order ODE for
\mathbf{\Upsilon}
, which yields a second order ODE for its entries, given by (
3.37
) and the relation (
3.35
). Following the usual dressing method for RHPs, one would be tempted in obtaining a similar ODE for
\mathbf{\Upsilon}
in the
\zeta
variable, and from which a Lax pair whose compatibility condition should yield scalar ODEs/PDEs of interest. However, the key feature for obtaining (
3.34
) was the fact that the jump matrix
\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}=\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty}}
for
\mathbf{\Upsilon}
is independent of
\mathsf{x}
, and because it is not constant as a function of
\zeta
or
\mathsf{s}
it is not possible to obtain the ODE in the
\zeta
or
\mathsf{s}
variables.

To overcome this issue, we follow a modification of the dressing method
2
2

2

We thank Thomas Chouteau for introducing this technique to us.
.
Start by defining

\mathbf{W}_{0}=\mathrm{i}\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{p}\mathbf{E}_{21},

which is introduced so that

(3.41)

\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)^{-1}-\mathbf{W}_{0}=\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-1}),\quad\zeta\to\infty.

For later reference, we state the expansion

(3.42)

\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)^{-1}=\mathbf{W}_{0}+\frac{1}{\zeta}\mathbf{W}_{-1}+\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-2}),\quad\zeta\to\infty,

with

\mathbf{W}_{-1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{p}\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{p}&-\mathrm{i}\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{p}\\
\mathrm{i}\left(\mathsf{p}^{2}\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{p}-2\mathsf{p}\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{q}-\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{r}\right)&-\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{q}+\mathsf{p}\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{p}\end{pmatrix},

as well as

(3.43)

\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)^{-1}-\mathbf{W}_{0}\\
=\mathsf{a}(\zeta)\zeta^{\alpha}\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\log\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}(\zeta)(\mathbf{I}+\mathrm{i}\mathsf{p}\mathbf{E}_{21})\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty,0}(0)\mathbf{E}_{12}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty,0}(0)^{-1}(\mathbf{I}-\mathrm{i}\mathsf{p}\mathbf{E}_{21})+\mathcal{O}(1),\quad\zeta\to 0.

Moving further, introduce

(3.44)

\mathbf{V}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)^{-1}\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)-\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)^{-1}\mathbf{W}_{0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta),\quad\zeta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0],

so that

(3.45)

\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)=\mathbf{W}_{0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)+\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)\mathbf{V}(\zeta),\quad\zeta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0].

Then, the product
\mathbf{\Upsilon}\mathbf{V}\mathbf{\Upsilon}^{-1}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0]
, and thanks to (
3.42
)–(
3.43
),

(3.46)

\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)\mathbf{V}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)^{-1}=\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)^{-1}-\mathbf{W}_{0}=\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-1}),&\zeta\to\infty,\\
\mathcal{O}(1+\mathsf{a}(\zeta)\zeta^{\alpha}),&\zeta\to 0.\end{cases}

We now compute additive jump relations for
\mathbf{\Upsilon}\mathbf{V}\mathbf{\Upsilon}^{-1}
along
(-\infty,0)
. The matrix
\mathbf{W}_{0}
is constant and therefore it has no jumps, so that

\displaystyle(\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)\mathbf{V}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)^{-1})_{+}-(\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)\mathbf{V}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)^{-1})_{-}

\displaystyle=(\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)^{-1})_{+}-(\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)^{-1})_{-}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{+}(\zeta)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\zeta)^{-1}\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{+}(\zeta)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{+}(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{12}\mathbf{\Upsilon}_{+}(\zeta)^{-1}.

From (
3.35
), (
3.36
) and the identity
\det\mathbf{\Upsilon}\equiv 1
we compute
\mathbf{\Upsilon}\mathbf{E}_{12}\mathbf{\Upsilon}^{-1}
, finally obtaining

(3.47)

(\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)\mathbf{V}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)^{-1})_{+}-(\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)\mathbf{V}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)^{-1})_{-}=\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}(\zeta)\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}\Phi(\zeta)\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi(\zeta)&\Phi(\zeta)^{2}\\
(\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi(\zeta))^{2}&\mathrm{i}\Phi(\zeta)\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi(\zeta)\end{pmatrix},

where in the above and in what follows we recall that we understand
\Phi(\zeta)=\Phi_{+}(\zeta)
for
\zeta<0
.

The jump relation (
3.47
) yields a scalar additive RHP for
\mathbf{\Upsilon}\mathbf{V}\mathbf{\Upsilon}^{-1}
along the jump contour
(-\infty,0)
. When coupled with the boundary conditions (
3.46
) on the endpoints
\infty,0
, it can be uniquely solved using Plemelj’s formula. In order to verify such solution, we still need to understand the behavior of its jump, given by the right-hand side of (
3.47
), near the endpoints
\infty,0
, which we do next.

With
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty,0}
being the analytic function on the right-hand side of (
3.28
), denote

\Phi_{0}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty,0})_{11}.

A direct calculation using (
3.28
) and (
3.33
) shows that

\Phi(\zeta)\sim\Phi_{0}(\zeta)\zeta^{\alpha/2}\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}(\zeta)^{-1/2},\quad\zeta\to 0.

Therefore,

\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}(\zeta)\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}\Phi(\zeta)\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi(\zeta)&\Phi(\zeta)^{2}\\
(\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi(\zeta))^{2}&\mathrm{i}\Phi(\zeta)\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi(\zeta)\end{pmatrix}\sim\zeta^{\alpha}\frac{\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}(\zeta)}{\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}(\zeta)}\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}\Phi_{0}(\zeta)\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi_{0}(\zeta)&\Phi_{0}(\zeta)^{2}\\
(\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi_{0}(\zeta))^{2}&\mathrm{i}\Phi_{0}(\zeta)\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi_{0}(\zeta)\end{pmatrix},

and together with the fact that
\zeta\mapsto\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\log\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}(\zeta)
is bounded along the real axis (see (
2.13
)), we learn

(3.48)

\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}(\zeta)\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}\Phi(\zeta)\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi(\zeta)&\Phi(\zeta)^{2}\\
(\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi(\zeta))^{2}&\mathrm{i}\Phi(\zeta)\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi(\zeta)\end{pmatrix}=\mathcal{O}(1+\zeta^{\alpha}),\quad\zeta\to 0.

Likewise, from the behavior (
3.26
) we get the rough estimate
\Phi(\zeta)=\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-1/4})
as
\zeta\to-\infty
, and from the explicit expression for
\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}
in (
2.13
) we learn that

\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}(\zeta)\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}\Phi(\zeta)\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi(\zeta)&\Phi(\zeta)^{2}\\
(\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi(\zeta))^{2}&\mathrm{i}\Phi(\zeta)\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi(\zeta)\end{pmatrix}=\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-|\zeta|^{m}}),\quad\zeta\to-\infty.

This estimate is not necessarily uniform in
\mathsf{s}
but it is sufficient for our purposes.

These last two estimates show that the right-hand side of (
3.47
) is integrable over the negative axis, so its Cauchy transform is well-defined. They also ensure that this Cauchy transform satisfies both local behaviors (
3.46
). As a consequence, we finally learn that

\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)\mathbf{V}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Upsilon}^{-1}(\zeta)=\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}\Phi(\xi)\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi(\xi)&\Phi(\xi)^{2}\\
(\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi(\xi))^{2}&\mathrm{i}\Phi(\xi)\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi(\xi)\end{pmatrix}\frac{\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi}{\xi-\zeta},\quad\zeta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0].

Let us combine what we have so far. Equations (
3.34
) and (
3.45
) form a Lax pair,

\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)=\mathbf{B}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)=\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)\mathbf{V}(\zeta)+\mathbf{W}_{0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta).

The compatibility between these two equations reads

(3.49)

\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathbf{B}(\zeta)-\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\mathbf{W}_{0}+[\mathbf{B}(\zeta),\mathbf{W}_{0}]=[\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)\mathbf{V}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)^{-1},\mathbf{B}(\zeta)]+\partial_{\mathsf{x}}(\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)\mathbf{V}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)^{-1}).

The terms
\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{W}_{0}
are matrix-valued rational functions in
\zeta
, whereas
\mathbf{\Upsilon}\mathbf{V}\mathbf{\Upsilon}^{-1}
- albeit more complicated - admits an asymptotic expansion as
\zeta\to\infty
of the form

(3.50)

\mathbf{\Upsilon}(\zeta)\mathbf{V}(\zeta)\mathbf{\Upsilon}^{-1}(\zeta)\sim-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\zeta^{k}}\mathbf{V}_{-k},

with coefficients

\mathbf{V}_{-k}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}\Phi(\xi)\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi(\xi)&\Phi(\xi)^{2}\\
(\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi(\xi))^{2}&\mathrm{i}\Phi(\xi)\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\Phi(\xi)\end{pmatrix}\xi^{k-1}\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi,\qquad k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}.

These coefficients clearly satisfy
\tr\mathbf{V}_{-k}=0
for every
k
.

Expanding (
3.49
) at
\zeta=\infty
in powers of
\zeta
, we obtain a system of equations between the coefficients of
\mathbf{B},\mathbf{W}_{0}
and the coefficients
\mathbf{V}_{-k}
. The first two of such nontrivial identities read

(3.51)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &[\mathbf{V}_{-1},\mathbf{B}_{1}]+[\mathbf{B}_{0},\mathbf{W}_{0}]+\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathbf{B}_{0}-\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\mathbf{W}_{0}=0,\\
&[\mathbf{V}_{-1},\mathbf{B}_{0}]+[\mathbf{V}_{-2},\mathbf{B}_{1}]+\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\mathbf{V}_{-1}=0.\end{aligned}\right.

Looking at the
(1,1)
-entry of the first relation in (
3.51
), we obtain the identity

(3.52)

\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{p}=-\mathrm{i}(\mathbf{V}_{-1})_{12}=-\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\Phi(\xi)^{2}\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi.

The next step is to integrate this identity over the interval
(\mathsf{s},\infty)
, and then take an
x
-derivative of the result. As a consequence of Proposition
4.8
that will be proved later,

\mathsf{p}\to-\frac{4\alpha^{2}-1}{8\mathsf{x}}\quad\text{as}\quad\mathsf{s}\to+\infty.

Also thanks to Proposition
4.8
, we know that for some function
f
which is integrable in compacts of
(-\infty,0]
, grows with
\mathcal{O}(|\zeta|^{1/4})
as
