-\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathsf{s}\mathsf{x}}{2}&1\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\infty,0}(\zeta+\mathsf{s})

is analytic near
\zeta=-\mathsf{s}
and

\mathsf{a}
is as in (
3.7
).

Note that
(\widehat{\mathbf{\Phi}}_{\infty,1})_{12}=-\mathrm{i}(\mathsf{p}+\frac{\mathsf{s}\mathsf{x}}{2})
, we define, similar to (
3.33
),

(5.13)

\widehat{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathrm{i}\left(\mathsf{p}+\frac{\mathsf{s}\mathsf{x}}{2}\right)\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\widehat{\mathbf{\Phi}}_{\infty}(\zeta).

Thus,
\widehat{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
satisfies the ODE

(5.14)

\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\widehat{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\zeta)=\widehat{\mathbf{B}}(\zeta)\widehat{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\zeta),

where

(5.15)

\widehat{\mathbf{B}}(\zeta)=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathrm{i}\\
\mathrm{i}(\zeta+2\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{p}+\mathsf{s})&0\end{pmatrix}.

Since the jump matrix of
\widehat{\mathbf{\Phi}}_{\infty}
in (
5.8
) is independent of
\mathsf{s}
as well, we obtain from (
5.12
) that

(5.16)

\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\widehat{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\zeta)=\widehat{\mathbf{A}}(\zeta)\widehat{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}(\zeta),

where

(5.17)

\widehat{\mathbf{A}}(\zeta)=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
\mathrm{i}(\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{p}+\frac{\mathsf{x}}{2})&0\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{\zeta+\mathsf{s}}\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{a}&\mathsf{b}\\
\mathsf{c}&-\mathsf{a}\end{pmatrix}

for some functions
\mathsf{a}
,
\mathsf{b}
and
\mathsf{c}
depending on
\mathsf{x}
and
\mathsf{s}
. The compatibility condition
\partial^{2}_{\mathsf{s}\mathsf{x}}\widehat{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}=\partial^{2}_{\mathsf{x}\mathsf{s}}\widehat{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
yields the zero curvature equation

\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\widehat{\mathbf{B}}-\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\widehat{\mathbf{A}}=\widehat{\mathbf{A}}\widehat{\mathbf{B}}-\widehat{\mathbf{B}}\widehat{\mathbf{A}}.

Inserting (
5.15
) and (
5.17
) into the above equation and comparing the coefficients of
\mathcal{O}(1)
and
\mathcal{O}(1/(\zeta+s))
terms, we arrive at

(5.18)

\mathsf{b}=-\mathrm{i}\left(\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{p}+\frac{\mathsf{x}}{2}\right),\quad\mathsf{a}=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial^{2}_{\mathsf{s}\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{p}+\frac{1}{2}\right),\quad\mathsf{c}=\mathrm{i}\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{a}-2\mathsf{b}\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{p},

and

(5.19)

\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{c}=4\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{p}.

From (
5.18
), it is readily seen that

\mathsf{c}=\mathrm{i}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\partial^{3}_{\mathsf{s}\mathsf{x}\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{p}+2\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{p}\partial_{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{p}+\mathsf{x}\partial_{\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{p}\right).

This, together with (
5.19
), implies the PDE for
\mathsf{p}
claimed in Theorem
2.8
.
∎

6.
Asymptotic analysis of the RHP for OPs

Our effort so far was the in-depth study of the model problem introduced in Section
3.1
. Now we come back to our original model, that is, extracting large
n
information on the point process with joint distribution (
2.1
). In this section, we carry out the asymptotic analysis of the associated polynomials, and in this analysis the solution
\mathbf{\Psi}_{\tau}
of the model problem with admissible data, as well as its limit
\mathbf{\Psi}_{\infty}
, will play a central role.

6.1.
Equilibrium measures and related quantities

In the course of the asymptotic analysis, we will need certain properties of equilibrium measures that we now briefly review. The properties we now mention can be found, e.g., in
[
47
,
30
]
, see also
[
2
]
for the specific structure of Cauchy transforms of log equilibrium measures with hard edge.

The
equilibrium measure
of the interval
[0,+\infty)
in the external field
V
is the unique probability measure supported on
[0,+\infty)
that minimizes the functional

\mu\mapsto\iint\log\frac{1}{|x-y|}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathrm{d}\mu(y)+\int V(x)\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

over all Borel probability measures
\mu
supported on
[0,+\infty)
. This measure is uniquely characterized by the
Euler-Lagrange variational conditions:
there exists a constant
\ell_{V}\in\mathbb{R}
for which

(6.1)

\int\log\frac{1}{|x-y|}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V}(y)+\frac{1}{2}V(x)+\ell_{V}\begin{cases}=0,&x\in\supp\mu_{V},\\
\geq 0,&x\in[0,+\infty).\end{cases}

Under our assumptions, the equilibrium measure
\mu_{V}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, say
\mathrm{d}\mu_{V}(x)=\mu_{V}^{\prime}(x)\mathrm{d}x
, for some function
\mu^{\prime}_{V}
. The regularity conditions on
\mu_{V}
that were mentioned in Assumption
2.1
translate into the following precise conditions.

Assumption 6.1

(One-cut regularity condition)
.

We assume that the potential
V
is such that the following holds.

(i)

For some
a>0
,
\supp\mu_{V}=[0,a]
.

(ii)

The inequality in (
6.1
) is strict, that is,

(6.2)

\int\log\frac{1}{|x-y|}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V}(y)+\frac{1}{2}V(x)+\ell_{V}>0,\quad x\in(a,\infty).

(iii)

\mu^{\prime}_{V}(x)>0
for
x\in(0,a)
.

(iv)

For some constants
\kappa_{0},\kappa_{a}>0
, we have

\mu^{\prime}_{V}(x)=\frac{\kappa_{0}}{\sqrt{x}}(1+\mathcal{O}(x)),\;x\searrow 0,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mu^{\prime}_{V}(x)=\kappa_{a}\sqrt{a-x}(1+\mathcal{O}(x-a)),\;x\nearrow a.

For the next transformation, we need to consider quantities related to the equilibrium measure. For

C^{\mu_{V}}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{V}(s)}{s-z},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,a],

being the Cauchy transform of the equilibrium measure, we associate to it its
\phi
function,

(6.3)

\phi(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{0}^{z}\left(C^{\mu_{V}}(s)+\frac{1}{2}V^{\prime}(s)\right)\mathrm{d}s,\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,a],

with the path of integration starting on the upper half-plane. Under Assumption
6.1
, the Cauchy transform satisfies an algebraic equation of the form

\left(C^{\mu_{V}}(z)+\frac{V^{\prime}(z)}{2}\right)^{2}=\frac{z-a}{z}h_{V}(z)^{2},

for some polynomial
h_{V}
with
h_{V}(0),h_{V}(a)\neq 0
. Standard arguments show that

(6.4)

\phi_{+}(z)+\phi_{-}(z)=0,\;0<z<a,\quad\phi_{+}(z)-\phi_{-}(z)=2\pi\mathrm{i}\mu_{V}([0,z])\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}_{+},\quad 0<z<a.

In particular,
h_{V}(x)<0
on
(0,a)
, and

\mu^{\prime}_{V}(x)=\frac{1}{\pi}\sqrt{\frac{a-x}{x}}|h_{V}(x)|,\quad 0<x<a.

Furthermore, a combination of (
6.4
), Cauchy-Riemann equations and the inequality (
6.2
) yields that

(6.5)

\phi(x)>0\;\text{for }x>a,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\re\phi(z)<0\text{ for }z\text{ slightly above or below }(0,a).

A local analysis based on conformal properties of
\phi
shows that for some
\delta>0
,

\re\phi(z)<0\text{ for }z\in D_{\delta}\setminus[0,\delta)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\phi(z)<0\text{ for }-\delta<z<0.

Consequently, the function

\psi(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{4}\phi(z)^{2}

is a conformal map in a neighborhood of the origin, and the behavior near the origin in Assumption
6.1
–(iv) ensures that

(6.6)

\psi(z)=-\mathsf{c}_{V}z(1+\mathcal{O}(z)),\quad z\to 0,\quad\text{for }\mathsf{c}_{V}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\pi^{2}\kappa_{0}^{2},

and where we recall that
\kappa_{0}>0
is as in Assumption
6.1
–(iv). This definition of
\mathsf{c}_{V}
is consistent with (
2.17
).

Finally, a direct calculation shows that, as
z\to\infty
,

(6.7)

\phi(z)=\frac{V(z)}{2}+\ell_{V}-\log z+\frac{\phi_{\infty}}{z}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-2}),

for some
\phi_{\infty}\in\mathbb{R}
, where
\ell_{V}
is as in Assumption
6.1
–(ii).

6.2.
The RHP for OPs

We start with the RHP for the corresponding OPs.

RHP 6.2
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{Y}:\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
with the following properties.

(i)

\mathbf{Y}:\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
is analytic.

(ii)

The matrix
\mathbf{Y}
has continuous boundary values
\mathbf{Y}_{\pm}
along
(0,+\infty)
, and they are related by
\mathbf{Y}_{+}(x)=\mathbf{Y}_{-}(x)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{Y}}(x)
,
x>0
, with

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{Y}}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{I}+\omega_{n}(x)\mathbf{E}_{12},\quad x>0.

(iii)

As
z\to\infty
,

\mathbf{Y}(z)=\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{1}{z}\mathbf{Y}_{1}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-2})\right)z^{n\bm{\sigma}_{3}}.

(iv)

As
z\to 0
,

\mathbf{Y}(z)=\mathcal{O}\begin{pmatrix}1&h_{\alpha}(z)\\
1&h_{\alpha}(z)\end{pmatrix},

where the
\mathcal{O}
-term is to be read entry-wise and here and from now on we set

(6.8)

h_{\alpha}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}z^{\alpha},&\alpha<0,\\
\log z,&\alpha=0,\\
1,&\alpha>0.\end{cases}

The weight
\omega_{n}(x)=\omega_{n}(x\mid\mathsf{s})
was introduced explicitly in (
2.2
) and depends on
\mathsf{s}
, and consequently so does
\mathbf{Y}(z)=\mathbf{Y}(z\mid\mathsf{s})
and any other quantity associated to the orthogonal polynomials, but sometimes we omit this dependence from our notation.

As it is now classical (see
[
34
]
), the solution to this RHP is related to the monic OP
\mathsf{P}_{j}
of degree
j
for the weight
\omega_{n}
via

\mathbf{Y}_{11}(z)=\mathsf{P}_{n}(z)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{Y}_{21}(z)=-2\pi\mathrm{i}(\gamma_{n-1}^{(n)})^{2}\mathsf{P}_{n-1}(z).

As a consequence of the Christoffel-Darboux formula,

(6.9)

\mathsf{K}_{n}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}(x-y)}\mathbf{e}_{2}^{T}\mathbf{Y}_{+}(y)^{-1}\mathbf{Y}_{+}(x)\mathbf{e}_{1},\;x\neq y,\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{K}_{n}(x,x)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\mathbf{e}_{2}^{T}\mathbf{Y}_{+}(x)^{-1}\mathbf{Y}_{+}^{\prime}(x)\mathbf{e}_{1},

where
\mathbf{e}_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(1,0)^{T}
and
\mathbf{e}_{2}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(0,1)^{T}
are the canonical base vectors for
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Using the explicit form

\partial_{u}\omega_{n}(x\mid u)=\ee^{-u-n^{2m}Q(x)}\sigma_{n}(x\mid u)\omega_{n}(x\mid u)=\omega_{n}(x\mid u)\partial_{u}\log\sigma_{n}(x\mid u),

the identity given by (
2.12
) updates to

\displaystyle\log\mathsf{L}_{n}^{Q}(\mathsf{s})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathsf{s}}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left[\mathbf{Y}(x\mid u)^{-1}\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}(x\mid u)\right]_{21,+}x^{\alpha}\ee^{-nV(x)}\partial_{u}\sigma_{n}(x\mid u)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}u

(6.10)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathsf{s}}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left[\mathbf{Y}(x\mid u)^{-1}\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}(x\mid u)\right]_{21,+}\omega_{n}(x\mid u)\partial_{u}\log\sigma_{n}(x\mid u)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}u.

This deformation formula will be the starting point for our asymptotic analysis of
\mathsf{L}_{n}^{Q}(\mathsf{s})
, but first we need to obtain asymptotics for
\mathbf{Y}
.

To obtain asymptotics for
\mathbf{Y}
we once again employ the Deift-Zhou nonlinear steepest descent method. The transformations needed in this case are now canonical, so we go over them briefly. When compared with more standard situations the only major obstacle is the construction of a local parametrix near the origin, which appears in virtue of the factor
\sigma_{n}
which scales singularly near the origin. In this construction, we will use the model problem that we previously studied in Sections
3
–
5
.

6.3.
First transformation: introduction of the
g
-function

The first transformation consists in introducing the
g
-function, which here is replaced by the
\phi
-function from (
6.3
).

Transform

\mathbf{T}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\ee^{-n\ell_{V}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{Y}(z)\ee^{n(\phi(z)-V(z)/2)\bm{\sigma}_{3}},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,\infty).

Then
\mathbf{T}
satisfies the following RHP.

RHP 6.3
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{Y}:\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
with the following properties.

(i)

\mathbf{T}:\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
is analytic.

(ii)

The matrix
\mathbf{T}
has continuous boundary values
\mathbf{T}_{\pm}
along
(0,+\infty)
, and they are related by
\mathbf{T}_{+}(x)=\mathbf{T}_{-}(x)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{T}}(x)
,
x>0
, with

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{T}}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{pmatrix}\ee^{n(\phi_{+}(x)-\phi_{-}(x))}&\sigma_{n}(x)x^{\alpha}\ee^{-n(\phi_{+}(x)+\phi_{-}(x))}\\
0&\ee^{-n(\phi_{+}(x)-\phi_{-}(x))}\end{pmatrix},\quad x>0.

(iii)

As
z\to\infty
,

\mathbf{T}(z)=\mathbf{I}+\frac{1}{z}\mathbf{T}_{1}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-2}).

with

\mathbf{T}_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\ee^{-n\ell_{V}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{Y}_{1}\ee^{n\ell_{V}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}+n\phi_{\infty}\bm{\sigma}_{3}.

(iv)

As
z\to 0
,

\mathbf{T}(z)=\mathcal{O}\begin{pmatrix}1&h_{\alpha}(z)\\
1&h_{\alpha}(z)\end{pmatrix},

where we recall that
h_{\alpha}
is given in (
6.8
).

6.4.
Second transformation: opening of lenses

0
a
\mathcal{L}^{+}
\mathcal{L}^{-}

Figure 2
.
The contour
\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}
for RHP
6.4
.

The next step is to open lenses
\mathcal{L}^{\pm}
around the interval
[0,a]
. For that, we modify

\mathbf{S}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{T}(z)\left(\mathbf{I}\mp\frac{z^{-\alpha}\ee^{2n\phi(z)}}{\sigma_{n}(z)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right),&z\in\mathcal{L}^{\pm},\\
\mathbf{T}(z),&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

In the above and what follows,
z^{\alpha}
is always the principal branch, with branch cut on
(-\infty,0]
.
Introduce the new contour

\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=[0,\infty)\cup\partial\mathcal{L}^{+}\cup\partial\mathcal{L}^{-}.

Using the properties of the function
\phi
discussed in Section
6.1
, in particular the jump relations (
6.4
), it follows that
the matrix
\mathbf{S}
solves the following RHP.

RHP 6.4
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{S}:\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
with the following properties.

(i)

\mathbf{S}:\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
is analytic.

(ii)

The matrix
\mathbf{S}
has continuous boundary values
\mathbf{S}_{\pm}
along the open subarcs of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}
, and they are related by
\mathbf{S}_{+}(z)=\mathbf{S}_{-}(z)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{S}}(z)
,
z\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}\setminus\{0,a\}
, with

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{S}}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}z^{\alpha}\sigma_{n}(z)\mathbf{E}_{12}-\frac{z^{-\alpha}}{\sigma_{n}(z)}\mathbf{E}_{21},&0<z<a,\\
\mathbf{I}+\frac{z^{-\alpha}}{\sigma_{n}(z)}\ee^{2n\phi(z)}\mathbf{E}_{21},&z\in\partial\mathcal{L}^{\pm}\setminus\mathbb{R},\\
\mathbf{I}+\sigma_{n}(z)z^{\alpha}\ee^{-2n\phi(z)}\mathbf{E}_{12},&z>a.\end{cases}

(iii)

As
z\to\infty
,

\mathbf{S}(z)=\mathbf{I}+\frac{1}{z}\mathbf{S}_{1}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-2}).

with
\mathbf{S}_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{T}_{1}
.

(iv)

As
z\to 0
, the matrix
\mathbf{S}
has the behavior

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}(z)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}\begin{pmatrix}1&h_{\alpha}(z)\\
1&h_{\alpha}(z)\end{pmatrix},&\text{outside the lens,}\\
\mathcal{O}\begin{pmatrix}h_{-\alpha}(z)&h_{\alpha}(z)\\
h_{-\alpha}(z)&h_{\alpha}(z)\end{pmatrix},&\text{inside the lens,}\\
\end{cases}

with
h_{\alpha}
given in (
6.8
).

Thanks to (
6.5
), we
have that
\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{S}}(z)\to\mathbf{I}
for
z\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}\setminus[0,a]
in an appropriate sense, and we now proceed to the construction of global and local parametrices. These parametrices are matrix-valued functions that satisfy the jumps for
\mathbf{S}
in the interval
(0,a)
and in neighborhoods of
z=0,a
, and will produce good approximations for
\mathbf{S}
near these sets.

6.5.
The global parametrix

The global parametrix RHP is obtained after neglecting the exponentially small jumps of
\mathbf{S}
. Concretely, the global parametrix
\mathbf{G}
is the solution to the following RHP.

RHP 6.5
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{G}:\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,a]\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
with the following properties.

(i)

\mathbf{G}:\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,a]\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
is analytic.

(ii)

The matrix
\mathbf{G}
has continuous boundary values
\mathbf{G}_{\pm}
along
[0,a]
, and they are related by

\mathbf{G}_{+}(z)=\mathbf{G}_{-}(z)\left(z^{\alpha}\sigma_{n}(z)\mathbf{E}_{12}-\frac{z^{-\alpha}}{\sigma_{n}(z)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right),\quad 0<z<a.

(iii)

As
z\to\infty
,

\mathbf{G}(z)=\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}).

(iv)

The matrix
\mathbf{G}
has at worse
L^{2}
-integrable singularities at
z=0,a
.

The construction of the solution
\mathbf{G}
is standard, and we now describe it. As we will see,
\mathbf{G}
depends on
\sigma_{n}
, so in fact
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{G}_{n}
, and we will also need to understand the behavior of
\mathbf{G}_{n}
as
n\to\infty
, which we also describe in this section.

The solution
\mathbf{G}
is explicitly given by

(6.11)

\mathbf{G}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\ee^{-\mathsf{p}_{0}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathsf{D}_{\infty}^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{M}(z)\mathsf{D}(z)^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\ee^{\mathsf{p}(z)\bm{\sigma}_{3}},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,a],

where each of the quantities is as follows. The function
\mathsf{D}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,a]
, and satisfies

\mathsf{D}_{+}(z)\mathsf{D}_{-}(z)=z^{\alpha}\text{ for }z\in(0,a),\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{D}(z)\to\mathsf{D}_{\infty}\neq 0\text{ as }z\to\infty.

With standard methods, we find

\mathsf{D}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{z^{\alpha/2}}{\mathsf{d}(z)^{\alpha/2}},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,a],

where
\mathsf{d}(z)
is the conformal map from
\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,a]
to the exterior of the unit disk, normalized to satisfy
\lim_{x\to+\infty}x^{-1}\mathsf{d}(x)=\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}\mathsf{d}_{\infty}>0
. A simple calculation shows that

\mathsf{d}(z)=\frac{2}{a}z-1+\frac{2}{a}z^{1/2}\left(z-a\right)^{1/2},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,a],

with principal branch of the root, so that
\mathsf{d}(z)=4z/a+\mathcal{O}(1)
as
z\to\infty
and therefore
\mathsf{d}_{\infty}=4/a
. Thus,
\mathsf{D}_{\infty}=2^{-\alpha}a^{\alpha/2}
.

The function
\mathsf{p}(z)
solves the scalar RHP

\mathsf{p}_{+}(z)+\mathsf{p}_{-}(z)=-\log\sigma_{n}(z),\quad 0<z<a,

with behavior

\mathsf{p}(z)=\mathcal{O}(1),\quad z\to 0,a,\infty.

With standard methods, we find

(6.12)

\mathsf{p}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{(z(z-a))^{1/2}}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{a}\frac{\log(\sigma_{n}(x))}{\sqrt{|x(x-a)|}}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{x-z},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,a],

with principal branch of the square root.
The constant
\mathsf{p}_{0}
is determined from

\mathsf{p}(z)=\mathsf{p}_{0}+\frac{\mathsf{p}_{1}}{z}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-2}),\quad z\to\infty,

from which we find

\mathsf{p}_{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{a}\frac{\log(\sigma_{n}(x))}{\sqrt{|x(x-a)|}}\mathrm{d}x,\quad\mathsf{p}_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{a}\frac{x\log(\sigma_{n}(x))}{\sqrt{|x(x-a)|}}\mathrm{d}x+\frac{a}{2}\mathsf{p}_{0}.

For

\mathbf{U}_{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathrm{i}\\
\mathrm{i}&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathsf{g}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{z-a}{z},

the matrix
\mathbf{M}
is

(6.13)

\mathbf{M}(z)=\mathbf{U}_{0}\mathsf{g}(z)^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}^{-1},

which solves the jump

\mathbf{M}_{+}(z)=\mathbf{M}_{-}(z)(\mathbf{E}_{12}-\mathbf{E}_{21}).

The function
\mathsf{p}=\mathsf{p}(\cdot\mid n)
and the constant
\mathsf{p}_{0}=\mathsf{p}_{0}(n)
both vary with
n
, but we mostly omit this dependence in our notation. It is however important to establish its behavior as
n\to\infty
. Using Proposition
B.1
we estimate these terms, the result is summarized in the next proposition.

Proposition 6.6
.

Set

\mathsf{p}_{\infty}(\mathsf{s})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{a^{-1/2}\mathsf{t}^{-1/(2m)}}{2\pi m}\mathsf{F}_{\frac{1}{2m}-1}(\mathsf{s}),

where
\mathsf{F}_{\beta}(\mathsf{s})=-\beta\Gamma(\beta)\Li_{\beta+2}(-\ee^{-\mathsf{s}})
with
\Li_{\beta}
being the polylogarithms. For any
\mathsf{s}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
fixed, the estimate

\mathsf{p}_{0}=\frac{1}{n}\mathsf{p}_{\infty}(\mathsf{s})+\mathcal{O}(n^{-1-2m}),\quad n\to\infty,

holds uniformly for
\mathsf{s}\geq\mathsf{s}_{0}
, and the estimate

\mathsf{p}(z)=\left(\frac{z-a}{z}\right)^{1/2}\frac{1}{n}\mathsf{p}_{\infty}(\mathsf{s})+\mathcal{O}(n^{-1-2m}),\quad n\to\infty

holds uniformly for
\mathsf{s}\geq\mathsf{s}_{0}
, and also uniformly for
z
in compacts of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,a\}
, where for
z\in(0,a)
it should be understood for boundary values
\mathsf{p}_{\pm}(z),\mathsf{p}_{\infty,\pm}
.

Proof.

We may estimate
\mathsf{p}_{0}
and
\mathsf{p}
using Proposition
B.1
–(ii), once we identify
\beta=-\frac{1}{2}
,
Q(x)=tq(x)
, and respectively
f(x)=|x-a|^{-1/2}
and
f(x)=|x-a|^{-1/2}(x-z)^{-1}
(or rather we look at the real and imaginary part of
f
). In the case that
z\in(0,a)
, the reader may verify that the (half) residue contribution corresponding to
x=z
to the integral defining
\mathsf{p}(z)
does not contribute to the dominant order, and one may effectively apply Proposition
B.1
–(ii) for some
\delta<z
. We obtain the stated result after invoking Proposition
B.1
–(i).
∎

As a consequence, we have the following proposition.

Proposition 6.7
.

The global parametrix
\mathbf{G}
defined in (
6.11
) satisfies

\mathbf{G}(z)=\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(n^{-1-2m})\right)\mathsf{D}_{\infty}^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{M}(z)\mathsf{D}(z)^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}},\quad n\to\infty,

uniformly for
z
in compacts of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{a,0\}
, where for
z\in(0,a)
this relation should be understood as valid (uniformly) for either of the
\pm
-boundary values. In particular, the boundary values
\mathbf{G}_{\pm}
remain uniformly bounded on compacts of
(0,a)
.

Proof.

Proposition
6.6
ensures that as
n\to\infty
,

\ee^{-\mathsf{p}_{0}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}=\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(n^{-1-2m}),\quad\text{as well as}\quad\ee^{\mathsf{p}(z)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}=\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(n^{-1-2m}),

with the latter being valid uniformly for
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,a\}
in compacts. Because
\mathsf{D}_{\infty},\mathsf{D}
and
\mathbf{M}
are independent of
n
and are bounded on compacts of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,a\}
, we then have that

\mathsf{D}_{\infty}^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{M}(z)\mathsf{D}(z)^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\ee^{\mathsf{p}(z)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}=\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(n^{-1-2m})\right)\mathsf{D}_{\infty}^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{M}(z)\mathsf{D}(z)^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}},

and the result follows from the explicit expression (
6.11
).
∎

With the global parametrix now constructed, we move forward to the local parametrices.

6.6.
The local parametrix near the hard edge

The local parametrix
\mathbf{P}^{(0)}
, constructed in a neighborhood
U_{0}
of
z=0
, reads as follows.

RHP 6.8
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{P}^{(0)}:U_{0}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
with the following properties

(i)

\mathbf{P}^{(0)}
is analytic on
U_{0}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}
.

(ii)

It has jump
\mathbf{P}^{(0)}_{+}(z)=\mathbf{P}^{(0)}_{-}(z)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{S}}(z)
,
z\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}\cap U_{0}
.

(iii)

For
z\in\partial U_{0}
, it behaves as

\mathbf{P}^{(0)}(z)=(\mathbf{I}+\mathit{o}(1))\mathbf{G}(z),\quad n\to\infty.

Making the change

(6.14)

\mathbf{L}(z)=\mathbf{P}^{(0)}(z)\ee^{-n\phi(z)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}(-z)^{\alpha\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2},\quad z\in U_{0}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}},

with the principal branch of the root
(\cdot)^{\alpha/2}
, it follows that
\mathbf{L}
should satisfy the following RHP.

RHP 6.9
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{L}:U_{0}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
with the following properties.

(i)

\mathbf{L}
is analytic on
U_{0}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}
.

(ii)

It has jump
\mathbf{L}_{+}(z)=\mathbf{L}_{-}(z)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{L}}(z)
,
z\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}\cap U_{0}
, with

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{L}}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\sigma_{n}(z)\mathbf{E}_{12}-\frac{1}{\sigma_{n}(z)}\mathbf{E}_{21},&z>0,\\
\mathbf{I}+\frac{\ee^{\mp\pi\alpha\mathrm{i}}}{\sigma_{n}(z)}\mathbf{E}_{21},&z\in(\partial\mathcal{L}^{\pm}\cap U_{0})\setminus[0,\infty),\\
\end{cases}

(iii)

For
z\in\partial U_{0}
, it behaves as

\mathbf{L}(z)=(\mathbf{I}+\mathit{o}(1))\mathbf{G}(z)(-z)^{\alpha\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\ee^{-n\phi(z)\bm{\sigma}_{3}},\quad n\to\infty.

The next step is to use the conformal map
\psi
from (
6.6
) to translate the RHP in the
z
-plane to a RHP in the transformed plane. For that, we look at

\zeta\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=n^{2}\psi(z),\quad\text{that is,}\quad z=\psi^{-1}\left(\frac{\zeta}{n^{2}}\right),

as a conformal change of variables. This correspondence satisfies

z<0\quad\text{ if, and only if, \quad}\zeta>0.

This way, it follows that
2\zeta^{1/2}=2(n^{2}\psi(z))^{1/2}=n(\phi(z)^{2})^{1/2}=\pm n\phi(z)
for the principal branch of the log and some choice of the sign. Because
\phi(z)<0
for
z<0
, we have in fact

2\zeta^{1/2}=-n\phi(z).

We also have to account for the transformation of the jump from the
z
-plane to the
\zeta
-plane. For that, for
\psi^{-1}
being the inverse of
\psi
, we take
\delta>0
such that
\psi^{-1}
is analytic on the disk
D_{2\delta}(0)
in the
w
plane, and such that
\psi(U_{0})=D_{\delta}(0)
. We also make sure that the lips of the lenses are mapped to the contours
\Gamma^{\pm}
from (
3.1
).

Introduce the new function

\mathsf{H}^{Q}(w)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=Q(\psi^{-1}(w)),\quad|w|<\delta,

which is an analytic function on
D_{\delta}(0)
that satisfies

\mathsf{H}^{Q}(w)=(-1)^{m}\frac{\mathsf{t}}{\mathsf{c}_{V}^{m}}w^{m}(1+\mathcal{O}(w)),\quad w\to 0.

In particular, the function

(6.15)

\mathsf{h}_{n}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathsf{s}+n^{2m}Q(z)=\mathsf{s}+n^{2m}Q\left(\psi^{-1}\left(\frac{\zeta}{n^{2}}\right)\right)=\mathsf{s}+n^{2m}\mathsf{H}^{Q}\left(\frac{\zeta}{n^{2}}\right),

is analytic on
|\zeta|\leq\delta n^{2}
and satisfies

\mathsf{h}_{n}(\zeta)=\mathsf{s}+(-1)^{m}\mathsf{u}\zeta^{m}+\mathcal{O}(n^{-2}),\quad n\to\infty,\qquad\mathsf{u}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\mathsf{t}}{\mathsf{c}_{V}^{m}}>0,

uniformly for
\zeta
in compacts of
\mathbb{C}
, or also the more refined estimate

\mathsf{h}_{n}(\zeta)=\mathsf{s}+(-1)^{m}\mathsf{u}\zeta^{m}+\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{m+1}n^{-2}),\quad n\to\infty,

which is valid for
|\zeta|\leq\delta n^{2}
.

We extend
\mathsf{H}^{Q}
in a
C^{\infty}
way to a neighborhood of
\Gamma
, so that with the identification
\tau=n
, this function
\mathsf{h}_{n}
is admissible in the sense of Definition
4.1
. In particular, from the considerations above and (
4.29
),

(6.16)

\left|\sigma_{n}(x)-\sigma_{\infty}(\zeta(x))\right|=\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\mathsf{s}}n^{-2}),\quad n\to\infty,\qquad\sigma_{\infty}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}_{\infty}(\zeta)}},

uniformly for
\zeta
in compacts of
\mathbb{C}
, where we recall that
\mathsf{h}_{\infty}(\zeta)=\mathsf{s}+(-1)^{m}\mathsf{u}\zeta^{m}
is as in (
3.21
).

Moving forward with the construction for
\mathbf{L}
, set

(6.17)

\mathbf{E}_{n}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{E}_{1}(z)n^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2},\quad\mathbf{E}_{1}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathsf{D}_{\infty}^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{M}(z)(-z)^{\alpha\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\mathsf{D}(z)^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{U}_{0}(\psi(z))^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4},\quad z\in U_{\delta}.

A direct calculation from its definition shows that
\mathbf{E}_{1}
is analytic near
z=0
.

With all these definitions, we construct the solution
\mathbf{L}
in the form

(6.18)

\mathbf{L}(z)=\mathbf{E}_{n}(z)\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{n}(z),\qquad\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{n}(z)=\bm{\sigma}_{3}\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}(\zeta)\bm{\sigma}_{3},\quad\zeta=\zeta(z)=n^{2}\psi(z),

where
\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}
should satisfy the following RHP.

RHP 6.10
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}:\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
with the following properties.

(i)

\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma
.

(ii)

The jump matrix
\mathbf{J}_{n}
for
\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}
is

\mathbf{J}_{n}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}+(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}_{n}(\zeta)})\ee^{\pi\alpha\mathrm{i}}\mathbf{E}_{21},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{1},\\
\frac{1}{1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}_{n}(\zeta)}}\mathbf{E}_{12}-(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}_{n}(\zeta)})\mathbf{E}_{21},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{2},\\
\mathbf{I}+(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}_{n}(\zeta)})\ee^{-\pi\alpha\mathrm{i}}\mathbf{E}_{21},&\zeta\in\Gamma_{3},\end{cases}

(iii)

As
\zeta\to\infty
,

(6.19)

\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}(\zeta)=\zeta^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-1/2})\right)\ee^{2\zeta^{1/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}},

where the error term is valid uniformly for
|\zeta|>R
, for some
R>0
independent of
n
.

A direct comparison shows that, with the identification
n=\tau
, the solution
\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}
of the model RHP
3.1
is a solution
\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}
of this RHP. In fact, RHP
6.10
(i) and (ii) are in direct correspondence with RHP
3.1
, and a direct calculation shows that RHP
3.1
–(iii) implies RHP
6.10
–(iii) (see also Remark
4.21
). From now on, we refer to this choice
\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}
as being the solution to RHP
6.10
, even though RHP
6.10
as posed may admit more than one solution
3
3

3

To ensure unique solution, we would have to specify the behavior as
\zeta\to 0
, but for clarity of presentation we opt for the approach presented without this condition a priori, only a posteriori
.

In particular, Proposition
3.2
ensures that

(6.20)

\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}(\zeta)=\mathbf{\Psi}^{(n)}_{0}(\zeta)\zeta^{\alpha\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}(\mathbf{I}+\mathsf{a}(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{12})\left(\mathbf{I}-(\chi_{\mathcal{S}_{+}}(\zeta)\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}-\chi_{\mathcal{S}_{-}}(\zeta)\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha})\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\\
\times(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}_{n}(\zeta)})^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2},\;\zeta\to 0,

where
\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(n)}
is analytic near the origin.

With the found
\mathbf{L}
, we now recover
\mathbf{P}^{(0)}
from (
6.14
). We now collect some properties that will play some role later on.

Proposition 6.11
.

The following properties hold.

(i)

The matrix
\mathbf{E}_{1}
is independent of
n
, and it is analytic in a neighborhood of
z=0
.

(ii)

For any
\mathsf{s}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, the function
\mathbf{P}^{(0)}
satisfies

(6.21)

\mathbf{P}^{(0)}(z)=\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(n^{-1})\right)\mathbf{G}(z),\quad n\to\infty,

uniformly for
z\in\partial U_{\delta}
, and uniformly for
\mathsf{s}\geq\mathsf{s}_{0}
.

(iii)

The entries of the matrix
\mathbf{\Psi}_{n,\pm}(\zeta)^{\pm 1}
remain bounded as
n\to\infty
, uniformly for
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}
with
1/M\leq|\zeta|\leq M
and
\mathsf{s}\geq\mathsf{s}_{0}
for any
\mathsf{s}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and any
M>0
.

(iv)

For any
\mathsf{s}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
fixed, there exist
n_{0}>0
and
M>0
, for which the matrix
\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}^{(n)}
is analytic and uniformly bounded on
|\zeta|<1/M
, for any
n\geq n_{0}
and any
\mathsf{s}\geq\mathsf{s}_{0}
.

(v)

For any
\mathsf{s}_{0}>0
fixed, there exist
n_{0}>0
and
M>0
for which the expansion (
6.19
) is valid on
|\zeta|>M
, uniformly for
n\geq n_{0}
and
\mathsf{s}\geq\mathsf{s}_{0}
.

Proof.

From its explicit expression, it is immediate that
\mathbf{E}_{1}
is independent of
n
and it is analytic on
U_{\delta}\setminus[0,a]
. A straightforward calculation shows that, in addition, it has no jumps along
(0,a)
, so its singularity at
z=0
is isolated. From the behavior of its entries, we see that in fact this singularity is removable, and the analyticity of
\mathbf{E}_{1}
follows.

The estimate on
\mathbf{L}
claimed in (ii) follows from the correspondence
\zeta=n^{2}\psi(z)
, the RHP satisfied by
\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}
, namely RHP
3.1
–(iii), and Proposition
6.7
. We skip details.

The claims (iii) and (iv) are true if we replace
\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}
by
\mathbf{\Psi}_{\infty}
. Since
\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}\to\mathbf{\Psi}_{\infty}
uniformly on compacts, standard arguments show that they are true for
\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}
as well, as long as we take
n
sufficiently large.

Finally, claim (v) is the same as Remark
4.21
for
\mathbf{\Psi}_{\tau}=\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}
.
∎

These considerations finish the construction of the local parametrix.

6.7.
The local parametrix near the soft edge

The local parametrix
\mathbf{P}^{(a)}
near the soft edge
z=a
is constructed in the usual way. Its main building block is the RHP for the Airy function that we describe next.

Set

\Gamma_{\mathbf{A}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{R}\cup(\ee^{2\pi\mathrm{i}/3}\infty,0]\cup(\ee^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}/3}\infty,0],

with the orientation of the arcs on real axis to be the natural orientation induced by
\mathbb{R}
, and with the non-real arcs oriented from
\infty
to
0
. The Airy parametrix
\mathbf{A}
is the solution to the following RHP.

RHP 6.12
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{A}:\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{A}}\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
with the following properties.

(i)

\mathbf{A}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{A}}
.

(ii)

The jump condition
\mathbf{A}_{+}(\zeta)=\mathbf{A}_{-}(\zeta)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{A}}(\zeta)
,
\zeta\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{A}}
holds, where the jump matrix
\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{A}}
for
\mathbf{A}
is

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{A}}(\zeta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{12},&\zeta\in(0,\infty),\\
\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E}_{21},&\zeta\in(\ee^{\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i}/3},0),\\
\mathbf{E}_{12}-\mathbf{E}_{21},&\zeta\in(-\infty,0).\end{cases}

(iii)

As
\zeta\to\infty
,

(6.22)

\mathbf{A}(\zeta)=\mathbf{\zeta}^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(\zeta^{-3/2})\right)\ee^{-\frac{2}{3}\zeta^{3/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}.

The solution
\mathbf{A}
can be constructed explicitly using Airy functions
[
31
]
, but we will not need its explicit expression. We now explain how to use it in our framework to construct the local parametrix near
z=a
.

Since we are assuming
Q
to be analytic on a neighborhood of the positive real axis, the function
\sigma_{n}
admits an analytic continuation to a neighborhood of
z=a
. Furthermore, its poles are the solutions to

Q(z)=-\frac{\mathsf{s}}{n^{2m}}+\frac{(2k+1)\pi\mathrm{i}}{n^{2m}}.

In particular, assuming that
\mathsf{s}\geq-\mathsf{s}_{0}
, with
\mathsf{s}_{0}>0
fixed, we see that there are no poles of
\sigma_{n}
near
z=a
. Furthermore,
Q(a)>0
, and therefore for the same range of values of
\mathsf{s}
,

\mathsf{s}+n^{2m}\re Q(z)\geq\eta n^{2m},

for some
\eta>0
, on any (small enough) neighborhood
D_{\delta}(a)
of
a
. In particular, this means that the functions
\sigma_{n}
and
\sigma_{n}^{-1}
both admit an analytic square root on a neighborhood
D_{\delta}(a)
independent of
n
and
\mathsf{s}
, and for this square root,

\sigma_{n}(z)^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}=\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta n^{2m}}),\quad n\to\infty,

uniformly for
z\in\overline{D}_{\delta}(a)
and also uniformly for
\mathsf{s}\geq-\mathsf{s}_{0}
.

Let us also introduce

(6.23)

\widetilde{\phi}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\phi(z)+\pi\mathrm{i}\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{\psi}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\left(\frac{3}{2}\widetilde{\phi}(z)\right)^{2/3}.

With standard arguments, we see that
\widetilde{\psi}
is a conformal map from a neighborhood of
z=a
to a neighborhood of the origin, and it maps
(a,a+\delta)
to the positive real axis. With this in mind, the local parametrix near
z=a
takes the form

(6.24)

\mathbf{P}^{(a)}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{E}^{(a)}_{n}(z)\mathbf{A}(n^{2/3}\widetilde{\psi}(z))\ee^{n\widetilde{\phi}(z)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\sigma_{n}(z)^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}z^{-\alpha\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2},

with

(6.25)

\mathbf{E}^{(a)}_{n}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{E}^{(a)}_{1}(z)n^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/6},\quad\mathbf{E}^{(a)}_{1}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathsf{D}_{\infty}^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{M}(z)\mathsf{D}(z)^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}}z^{\alpha\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\mathbf{U}_{0}^{-1}\widetilde{\psi}(z)^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}.

Also, it is straightforward to show that this factor
\mathbf{E}^{(a)}_{1}
is analytic near
z=a
, and that the matching

(6.26)

\mathbf{P}^{(a)}(z)=(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(n^{-1}))\mathbf{G}(z),\quad n\to\infty,\quad z\in\partial U_{a}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=D_{\delta}(a),

holds uniformly for
\mathsf{s}\geq-\mathsf{s}_{0}
with any fixed
\mathsf{s}_{0}>0
.

6.8.
Conclusion of the asymptotic analysis

We are ready to conclude the asymptotic analysis. Set

\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}\setminus\left(U_{0}\cup U_{a}\cup(0,a)\right),

and orient the parts
\partial U_{0}
and
\partial U_{0}
of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}}
in the clockwise direction. Let
\mathbf{P}^{(a)}
and
\mathbf{P}^{(0)}
be the local parametrices near
z=a
and
z=0
, respectively, and set

\mathbf{P}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{P}^{(0)}(z),&z\in U_{0},\\
\mathbf{P}^{(a)}(z),&z\in U_{a},\\
\mathbf{G}(z),&z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\left(\Gamma_{\mathbf{S}}\cup\overline{U}_{0}\cup\overline{U}_{a}\right).\end{cases}

Make the transformation

\mathbf{R}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{S}(z)\mathbf{P}(z)^{-1},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}}.

Then
\mathbf{R}
satisfies the following RHP.

RHP 6.13
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{R}:\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}}\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
with the following properties.

(i)

\mathbf{R}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}}
.

(ii)

The jump condition
\mathbf{R}_{+}(\zeta)=\mathbf{R}_{-}(\zeta)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{R}}(\zeta)
,
\zeta\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{A}}
holds, where

\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{R}}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{G}(z)\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{S}}(z)\mathbf{G}(z)^{-1},&z\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}}\setminus(\partial U_{0}\cup\partial U_{a}),\\
\mathbf{P}^{(0)}(z)\mathbf{G}(z)^{-1},&z\in\partial U_{0},\\
\mathbf{P}^{(a)}(z)\mathbf{G}(z)^{-1},&z\in\partial U_{a}.\end{cases}

(iii)

As
z\to\infty
,

\mathbf{R}(z)=\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}),

where the error term is valid uniformly for
|\zeta|>R
with some
R>0
independent of
n
.

With standard arguments, in particular thanks to (
6.21
) and (
6.26
), we obtain the next result.

Theorem 6.14
.

The matrix
\mathbf{R}
satisfies

\mathbf{R}(z)=\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(n^{-1}),\quad n\to\infty,

uniformly for
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}}
. Furthermore, this estimate holds for boundary values
\mathbf{R}_{\pm}
along
\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}}
, in
L^{\infty}
and
L^{1}
norms, uniformly for
\mathsf{s}\geq-\mathsf{s}_{0}
with any fixed
\mathsf{s}_{0}>0
.

This last result concludes the asymptotic analysis, and now we move on to deriving its consequences.

7.
Conclusion of main results

Now that the asymptotic analysis for orthogonal polynomials is complete, we are ready to draw its consequences, in particular proving our main results.

For reference throughout this section, we trace back all the transformations of the RH analysis for OPs, which yields

(7.1)

\mathbf{Y}(z)=\ee^{n\ell_{V}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{R}(z)\mathbf{P}(z)\left(\mathbf{I}+\left(\chi_{\mathcal{L}^{+}}(z)-\chi_{\mathcal{L}^{-}}(z)\right)\frac{z^{-\alpha}\ee^{2n\phi(z)}}{\sigma_{n}(z)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\ee^{-n(\phi(z)-V(z)/2)\bm{\sigma}_{3}},\quad z\in\mathbb{C},

where we recall that
\mathcal{L}^{\pm}
are the lenses used in the transformation
\mathbf{T}\mapsto\mathbf{S}
(see Figure
2
), and
\phi
is as in (
6.3
).

7.1.
Asymptotics for the kernel: proof of Theorem
2.4

To obtain the asymptotic behavior of the kernel, we follow a standard route in RHPs. However, in the case considered here, the local parametrix
\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}
depends on
n
in a nontrivial manner, so for completeness we decided to present the detailed argument.

Thanks to (
6.9
), the correlation kernel (
2.10
) can be expressed in terms of the solution of the RHP
6.2
for OPs as

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{K}}_{n}(x,y\mid s)=\frac{\sqrt{\omega_{n}(x\mid s)\omega_{n}(y\mid s)}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}(x-y)}\mathbf{e}_{2}^{T}\mathbf{Y}_{+}(y)^{-1}\mathbf{Y}_{+}(x)\mathbf{e}_{1}.

Next, stressing that we are interested in
x,y\in(0,\infty)
in a neighborhood of the hard edge, we plug (
7.1
) into this identity. Thanks to Theorem
6.14
, standard arguments show that

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}(y)^{-1}\mathbf{R}(x)=\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{x-y}{n}\right),

as
n\to\infty
, uniformly for
x,y
in compact sets of
\mathbb{C}
(in particular, the uniform convergence on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\mathbf{R}}
can be extended to compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}
). Thus,

\widehat{\mathsf{K}}_{n}(x,y)=\frac{(xy)^{\alpha/2}\sqrt{\sigma_{n}(x)}\sqrt{\sigma_{n}(y)}\ee^{-n(\phi(x)+\phi(y))}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}(x-y)}\\
\times\left[\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{y^{-\alpha}\ee^{2n\phi(y)}}{\sigma_{n}(y)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\mathbf{P}^{(0)}(y)^{-1}\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{x-y}{n}\right)\right)\mathbf{P}^{(0)}(x)\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{x^{-\alpha}\ee^{2n\phi(x)}}{\sigma_{n}(x)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\right]_{21,+},

valid uniformly for
x,y
in compacts of
(0,\infty)\cap U_{0}
, where we recall that
U_{0}
is the neighborhood where the local parametrix
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}^{(0)}
was constructed in Section
6.6
.

Using now the very construction of
\mathbf{P}^{(0)}
from (
6.18
) and (
6.14
), we simplify this last expression to

\widehat{\mathsf{K}}_{n}(x,y)=-\frac{\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}\sqrt{\sigma_{n}(x)}\sqrt{\sigma_{n}(y)}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}(x-y)}\Bigg[\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\sigma_{n}(y)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}(\zeta(y))^{-1}\bm{\sigma}_{3}n^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\mathbf{E}_{1}(y)^{-1}\\
\times\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{x-y}{n}\right)\right)\mathbf{E}_{1}(x)n^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}(\zeta(x))\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\sigma_{n}(x)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\Bigg]_{21,+},

The function
\mathbf{E}_{1}
is analytic near the origin and independent of
n
. Standard arguments show that

\mathbf{E}_{1}(y)^{-1}\mathbf{E}_{1}(x)=\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}(x-y).

Hence, setting from now on

\zeta\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\zeta(x)=n^{2}\psi(x),\quad\xi\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\zeta(y)=n^{2}\psi(y),

we update the expression above to

\widehat{\mathsf{K}}_{n}(x,y)=-\frac{\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}\sqrt{\sigma_{n}(x)}\sqrt{\sigma_{n}(y)}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}(x-y)}\\
\times\Bigg[\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\sigma_{n}(y)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}(\xi)^{-1}\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathcal{O}\left(x-y\right)\right)\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}(\zeta)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\sigma_{n}(x)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\Bigg]_{21,-}.

To obtain the formula above, we moved from a
+
-boundary value to a
-
-boundary value in virtue of the change of orientation coming from
x\mapsto\zeta
; see (
6.6
).
With
\mathsf{c}_{V}
being the constant in (
6.6
), let us scale

x=\frac{u}{\mathsf{c}_{V}n^{2}},\quad y=\frac{v}{\mathsf{c}_{V}n^{2}},

with
u,v>0
, so that, from (
6.6
),

\zeta(x)=-u+\mathcal{O}(n^{-2})\quad\text{and}\quad\xi=\zeta(y)=-v+\mathcal{O}(n^{-2}).

For
u,v
in compact subsets of the positive axis, the values
\zeta=\zeta(x)
and
\xi=\zeta(y)
remain uniformly in compact subsets of the negative axis, where the model problem
\mathbf{\Psi}_{n,-}
is uniformly bounded in
n
(see Proposition
6.11
–(iii)). Recalling also (
6.16
), we update this estimate to

\frac{1}{\mathsf{c}_{V}n^{2}}\widehat{\mathsf{K}}_{n}\left(\frac{u}{\mathsf{c}_{V}n^{2}},\frac{v}{\mathsf{c}_{V}n^{2}}\right)=\frac{\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}\sqrt{\widehat{\sigma}_{n}(-u)}\sqrt{\widehat{\sigma}_{n}(-v)}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}(v-u)}\\
\times\Bigg[\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\widehat{\sigma}_{n}(-v)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\mathbf{\Psi}_{\tau}(-v)^{-1}\mathbf{\Psi}_{\tau}(-u)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\widehat{\sigma}_{n}(-u)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\Bigg]_{21,-}+\mathcal{O}(n^{-2}).

where
\mathbf{\Psi}_{\tau}=\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}
and
\widehat{\sigma}_{n}(\zeta)=\sigma_{n}(x(\zeta))
is admissible and such that
\widehat{\sigma}_{n}(u)\to\sigma_{\infty}(u)=(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}_{\infty}(u)})^{-1}
.

Recalling that
\sigma_{\infty}(\zeta)=(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}_{\infty}(\zeta)})^{-1}
and (
6.16
), and applying Theorem
5.1
we conclude the proof of Theorem
2.4
.

7.2.
Asymptotics for the multiplicative statistics: proof of Theorem
2.6

In this section,
\partial
denotes the derivative with respect to the parameter
\mathsf{s}
, whereas
′
will be used for the derivative with respect to the spectral (the RHP) variable, so that for instance

\partial\sigma_{n}(x\mid\mathsf{s})=\frac{\partial\sigma_{n}}{\partial\mathsf{s}}(x\mid\mathsf{s})\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma^{\prime}_{n}(x\mid\mathsf{s})=\frac{\partial\sigma_{n}}{\partial x}(x\mid\mathsf{s}).

Let us choose the neighborhoods
U_{0}
and
U_{a}
where the local parametrices were constructed so that, to simplify notation,

U_{0}\cap(0,\infty)=(0,\delta)\quad\text{and}\quad U_{a}\cap(0,\infty)=(a-\delta,a+\delta).

We split (
6.10
) as

(7.2)

\log\mathsf{L}_{n}^{Q}(\mathsf{s})=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathsf{s}}^{\infty}\left(\mathsf{J}_{\rm h}(u)+\mathsf{J}_{\rm b}(u)+\mathsf{J}_{\rm s}(u)+\mathsf{J}_{\rm g}(u)\right)\mathrm{d}u,

where the terms
\mathsf{J}_{\rm h},\mathsf{J}_{\rm b},\mathsf{J}_{\rm s},\mathsf{J}_{\mathsf{g}}
correspond to the
x
-integrals over the neighborhood
(0,\delta)
of the hard edge region, over the bulk region
(\delta,a-\delta)
, over the neighborhood
(a-\delta,a+\delta)
of the soft edge, and over the gap interval
(a+\delta,\infty)
away from the support of
\mu_{V}
, respectively. We split the analysis of each such term in different sections below.

7.2.1.
Contributions near the hard edge

In this section we analyze the integral

(7.3)

\int_{\mathsf{s}}^{\infty}\mathsf{J}_{\rm h}(u)\mathrm{d}u,\quad\text{with}\quad\mathsf{J}_{\rm h}(\mathsf{s})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{0}^{\delta}\left[\mathbf{Y}(x\mid\mathsf{s})^{-1}\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}(x\mid\mathsf{s})\right]_{21,+}\omega_{n}(x\mid\mathsf{s})\partial\log\sigma_{n}(x\mid\mathsf{s})\mathrm{d}x,

which will turn out to give the leading contribution to (
7.2
).

The idea is standard: we unwrap the transformations
\mathbf{Y}\mapsto\cdots\mapsto\mathbf{R}
of the RHP analysis, with careful considerations along the way. When doing so, we will ultimately approximate
\mathbf{Y}
by the local parametrix
\mathbf{P}^{(0)}
near the hard edge, and this parametrix will give us the leading contribution.

For
x\in(0,\delta]
, that is, in the neighborhood where we constructed the hard edge parametrix, we start from (
7.1
) and use (
6.14
) and (
6.18
) to write

\mathbf{Y}_{+}(x)=\ee^{n\ell_{V}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{R}_{+}(x)\mathbf{E}_{n}(x)\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{n,+}(x)\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}\mathbf{E}_{21}}{\sigma_{n}(x)}\right)x^{-\alpha\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\ee^{nV(x)\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2},

where
\mathbf{E}_{n}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{n}
are as in (
6.18
) and (
6.17
).

With the notation
\mathbf{\Delta}_{x},\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}
introduced in (
2.24
)–(
2.25
) in mind, we compute

(7.4)

[\mathbf{Y}(x)^{-1}\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}(x)]_{21,+}=-\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha+nV(x)}x^{-\alpha}\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\left(\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}(\zeta(x))\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\sigma_{n}(x)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\right)\right]_{21,+}\\
-\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha+nV(x)}x^{-\alpha}\left[\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\sigma_{n}(x)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\mathbf{R}_{\rm h}(x)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\sigma_{n}(x)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\right]_{21,+},

which is valid for
x\in(0,\delta)
and where we have set

\mathbf{R}_{\rm h}(x)=\mathbf{R}_{\rm h}(x\mid\mathsf{s})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}={\mathbf{\Psi}}_{n}(\zeta(x))^{-1}\bm{\sigma}_{3}n^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\left(\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\mathbf{E}_{1}(x)+\mathbf{E}_{1}(x)^{-1}\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\mathbf{R}(x)\mathbf{E}_{1}(x)\right)n^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{n}(\zeta(x)).

Plugging the result into (
7.3
), we obtain the identity

(7.5)

\mathsf{J}_{\rm h}(u)=-\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}\int_{0}^{\delta}\zeta^{\prime}(x)\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\left(\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}(\zeta)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\sigma_{n}(x(\zeta)\mid u)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\right)\right]_{21,+}\partial\sigma_{n}(x\mid u)\mathrm{d}x\\
-\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}\int_{0}^{\delta}\left[\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\sigma_{n}(x\mid u)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\mathbf{R}_{{\rm h},+}(x)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\sigma_{n}(x\mid u)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\right]_{21}\partial\sigma_{n}(x\mid u)\mathrm{d}x.

We now obtain a bound for the integral on the second line. From Proposition
6.11
–(i) and Theorem
6.14
we estimate

(7.6)

n^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\mathbf{E}_{1}(x)n^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}=\mathcal{O}(n),\qquad\text{and}\qquad n^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\mathbf{E}_{1}(x)^{-1}\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\mathbf{R}(x)\mathbf{E}_{1}(x)n^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}=\mathcal{O}(1),

as
n\to\infty
, so that

(7.7)

\mathbf{R}_{\rm h}(x)={\mathbf{\Psi}}_{n}(\zeta(x))^{-1}\mathcal{O}(n){\mathbf{\Psi}}_{n}(\zeta(x)),\quad n\to\infty,

where the error term is valid uniformly for
x\in(0,\delta)
and uniformly for
\mathsf{s}\geq-\mathsf{s}_{0}
with any fixed
\mathsf{s}_{0}>0
.

To estimate the withstanding term
\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}
, we explore the change of variables
\zeta=n^{2}\psi(x)
, which maps the interval
[0,\delta]
to an interval of the form
[-R_{n},0]
, with
R_{n}=\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
. We split such interval in three pieces

[-R_{n},0]=[-R_{n},-M]\cup[-M,-1/M]\cup[-1/M,0],

where
M>0
is independent of
n
and chosen so that Proposition
6.11
–(iii),(iv),(v) are all valid. In what follows, recall also that
1/\sigma_{n}(z)=1+\ee^{\mathsf{h}_{n}(\zeta)}
, with
\mathsf{h}_{n}=\mathsf{h}_{\tau}
admissible in the sense of Definition
4.1
.

In the compact interval
[-M,-1/M]
the convergence
\ee^{-\mathsf{h}_{n}(\zeta)}\to\ee^{-\mathsf{s}+(-1)^{m+1}\mathsf{u}\zeta^{m}}
takes place uniformly, and this limit is bounded in this same interval, uniformly also for
\mathsf{s}\geq-\mathsf{s}_{0}
. Combining with Proposition
6.11
–(iii) and (
7.7
), we obtain

(7.8)

\left[\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\sigma_{n}(x)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\mathbf{R}_{\rm h}(x)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\sigma_{n}(x)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\right]_{21,+}=\mathcal{O}(n),\quad n\to\infty,

valid uniformly for
\zeta(x)\in[-M,-1/M]
and uniformly for
\mathsf{s}\geq-\mathsf{s}_{0}
.

On the interval
[-1/M,0]
, we use (
6.20
) and Proposition
6.11
–(iv) and write

{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{n,+}(\zeta(x))\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\sigma_{n}(x)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)=\mathcal{O}(1)\zeta^{\frac{\alpha}{2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}_{-}(\mathbf{I}+\mathsf{a}_{-}(\zeta)\mathbf{E}_{12})\sigma(x)^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2},\quad n\to\infty,

and therefore using again (
7.7
),

(7.9)

\left[\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\sigma_{n}(x)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\mathbf{R}_{\rm h}(x)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\sigma_{n}(x)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\right]_{21,+}=\mathcal{O}\left(n^{1+2\alpha}|x|^{\alpha}\right),\quad n\to\infty,

valid uniformly for
\zeta=\zeta(x)\in[-1/M,0]
and
\mathsf{s}\geq-\mathsf{s}_{0}
, and where we used that
\zeta=n^{2}\psi(x)
,
\psi
is conformal near the origin, and
\sigma_{n}
is bounded along the real axis.

Finally, on the interval
[-R_{n},-M]
we now use Proposition
6.11
–(v) and conclude that

{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{n,+}(\zeta(x))\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\sigma_{n}(x)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)=\\
\mathcal{O}(1)\zeta^{-\frac{1}{4}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}_{-}\mathbf{U}_{0}(\mathbf{I}-(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}_{n}(\zeta)})\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha-4\mathrm{i}|\zeta|^{1/2}}\mathbf{E}_{21})\ee^{-2\mathrm{i}|\zeta|^{1/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}},\quad\zeta(x)\in[-R_{n},-M],\quad n\to\infty,

also with uniform error term for
\mathsf{s}\geq-\mathsf{s}_{0}
, and emphasizing that this error term depends on the fixed value
M
but it is independent of
R_{n}
. As said before,
\mathsf{h}_{n}
is admissible, and Definition
4.1
–(iii) implies in particular that
(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}_{n}})
is bounded along the negative axis, uniformly for
\mathsf{s}\geq-\mathsf{s}_{0}
and uniformly in
n
as well. With this observation in mind, the last estimate and (
7.7
) together imply

(7.10)

\left[\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\sigma_{n}(x)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\mathbf{R}_{\rm h}(x)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\sigma_{n}(x)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\right]_{21,+}=\mathcal{O}(n^{2}(1+|x|^{1/2})),\quad n\to\infty,

uniformly for
\zeta=\zeta(x)\in[-R_{n},-M]
and
\mathsf{s}\geq-\mathsf{s}_{0}
. In fact,
x
is bounded in the corresponding set, and the error term above is effectively
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
.

Again having in mind that
\zeta=\zeta(x)=n^{2}\psi(x)
and that
\psi(x)
is an
n
-independent conformal map near the origin, we use (
7.8
),(
7.9
) and (
7.10
), obtaining that there exists a constant
C>0
for which the integral on the second line of (
7.5
) is bounded by

(7.11)

Cn\left(n^{2\alpha}\int_{0}^{\zeta^{-1}(-1/M)}\partial\sigma_{n}(x\mid u)x^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\zeta^{-1}(-1/M)}^{\zeta^{-1}(M)}\partial\sigma_{n}(x\mid u)\mathrm{d}x+n\int_{\zeta^{-1}(M)}^{\delta}\partial\sigma_{n}(x\mid u)\mathrm{d}x\right),

where the additional factor
n
in the last term comes from the factor
\zeta^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}=(n^{2}\psi(x))^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}
in (
7.10
). An explicit calculation shows that

0\leq\partial\sigma_{n}(x\mid\mathsf{s})=\frac{\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-n^{2m}Q(x)}}{(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-n^{2m}Q(x)})^{2}}\leq\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-n^{2m}Q(x)}.

Having in mind that
M>0
is fixed and
R_{n}=\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
, we obtain that (
7.10
) - and thus the second line in (
7.5
) - is bounded by

(7.12)

Cn\ee^{-u}\left(n^{2\alpha}\int_{0}^{1/\eta}x^{\alpha}\ee^{-n^{2m}Q(x)}\mathrm{d}x+n\int_{1/\eta}^{\eta}\ee^{-n^{2m}Q(x)}\mathrm{d}x\right)

for some
\eta,C>0
, which we emphasize may depend on
\mathsf{s}_{0}>0
fixed but it is independent of
\mathsf{s}\geq-\mathsf{s}_{0}
. Under our conditions on
Q
(recall Assumption
2.2
), it is immediate that

n\int_{1/\eta}^{\infty}\ee^{-n^{2m}Q(x)}\mathrm{d}x=\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta^{\prime}n^{2m}}),

for some
\eta^{\prime}>0
independent of
\mathsf{s}
, and the change of variables
n^{2}x=y
yields the estimate

n^{2\alpha}\int_{0}^{1/\eta}x^{\alpha}\ee^{-n^{2m}Q(x)}\mathrm{d}x=\mathcal{O}(n^{-2}).

Using these estimates in (
7.5
), we finally arrive at the estimate

(7.13)

\mathsf{J}_{\rm h}(u)=-\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}\int_{0}^{\delta}\zeta^{\prime}(x)\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\left(\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}(\zeta)\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\sigma_{n}(x(\zeta)\mid u)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\right)\right]_{21,+}\partial\sigma_{n}(x\mid u)\mathrm{d}x+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\ee^{-u}}{n}\right),

valid as
n\to\infty
, uniformly for
u\geq-\mathsf{s}_{0}
, for any
\mathsf{s}_{0}>0
. We now change variables
x\mapsto\zeta
in the integral. For that, notice that

\sigma_{n}(x(\zeta)\mid u)=\frac{1}{1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}_{n}(\zeta\mid u)}},

where
\mathsf{h}_{n}=\mathsf{h}_{\tau}
is admissible in the sense of Definition
4.1
. With
R=R_{n}
determined by relation

-n^{2}R_{n}=\zeta(\delta),

we know that
R_{n}>0
is bounded from above, and also from below away
0
, and this estimate becomes

(7.14)

\mathsf{J}_{\rm h}(u)=\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}\int_{-n^{2}R}^{0}\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{\zeta}\left[\mathbf{\Psi}_{n}(\zeta)\left(\mathbf{I}-\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}\left(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}_{n}(\zeta\mid u)}\right)\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\right]\right]_{21,-}\frac{\ee^{-\mathsf{h}_{n}(\zeta\mid u)}}{(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}_{n}(\zeta\mid u)})^{2}}\mathrm{d}\zeta+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\ee^{-u}}{n}\right),

valid as
n\to\infty
, uniformly for
u\geq-\mathsf{s}_{0}
, for any
\mathsf{s}_{0}>0
fixed.

Using now Theorem
5.2
, we obtain

(7.15)

\int_{\mathsf{s}}^{\infty}\mathsf{J}_{\rm h}(u)\mathrm{d}u=2\pi\mathrm{i}\int_{\mathsf{s}}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\mathsf{K}_{\alpha}(-\zeta,-\zeta\mid u)\frac{\ee^{-\mathsf{h}_{\infty}(\zeta\mid u)}}{1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}_{\infty}(\zeta\mid u)}}\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}u+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\ee^{-\mathsf{s}}}{n}\right),\quad n\to\infty,

uniformly for
\mathsf{s}\geq\mathsf{s}_{0}
.

7.2.2.
Contributions away from the support

Returning to the analysis of (
7.2
),
we now estimate

(7.16)

\int_{\mathsf{s}}^{\infty}\mathsf{J}_{\rm g}(u)\mathrm{d}u,\quad\text{with}\quad\mathsf{J}_{\rm g}(\mathsf{s})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{a+\delta}^{\infty}\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\mathbf{Y}(x\mid\mathsf{s})\right]_{21,+}\omega_{n}(x\mid\mathsf{s})\partial\log\sigma_{n}(x\mid\mathsf{s})\mathrm{d}x.

In the interval
[a+\delta,\infty)
, the unwrap of the transformations yields

\mathbf{Y}(z)=\ee^{n\ell_{V}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{R}(z)\mathbf{G}(z)\ee^{-n(\phi(x)-V(x)/2)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}.

Therefore, using the explicit expression for
\mathbf{G}
in (
6.11
) we obtain

\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\mathbf{Y}(x)\right]_{21,+}=\frac{\ee^{-2n\phi(x)+nV(x)+2\mathsf{p}(x)}}{\mathsf{D}(x)^{2}}\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\mathbf{M}(z)+\mathbf{M}(x)^{-1}\mathsf{D}_{\infty}^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\ee^{\mathsf{p}_{0}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\mathbf{R}_{+}(x)\ee^{-\mathsf{p}_{0}}\mathsf{D}_{\infty}^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{M}(x)\right]_{21}.

A direct calculation from (
6.13
) reveals that

(7.17)

\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\mathbf{M}(x)\right]_{21}=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\frac{\mathsf{g}^{\prime}(x)}{\mathsf{g}(x)}=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\frac{a}{x(x-a)},

which is clearly bounded on
[a+\delta,\infty)
. Likewise, all the entries of
\mathbf{M}
are bounded in this same interval, because so is
\mathsf{g}
. The term
\mathsf{D}
is discussed after (
6.11
), and it is continuous and nonzero on the interval
[a+\delta,\infty)
, and converges to
\mathsf{D}_{\infty}\neq 0
at
\infty
. Therefore the factor
\mathsf{D}(x)^{-2}
is also bounded on
[a+\delta,\infty)
. The terms
\mathsf{D}_{\infty}
and
\mathsf{p}_{0}
are constant in
x
, with the former being independent of
n
as well while the latter is uniformly bounded in
n
thanks to Proposition
6.6
. Finally, thanks to Theorem
6.14
we know that the factor
\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\mathbf{R}_{+}
is
\mathcal{O}(n^{-1})
.

All in all, we conclude that, with the exception of the exponential component, all terms on the right hand side of (
7.2.2
) are bounded, and therefore for some
M>0
,

\left|\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\mathbf{Y}(x\mid\mathsf{s})\right]_{21,+}\omega_{n}(x\mid\mathsf{s})\partial\log\sigma_{n}(x\mid\mathsf{s})\right|\leq\frac{\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-n^{2m}Q(x)}}{(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-n^{2m}Q(x)})^{2}}x^{\alpha}\ee^{-2n\phi(x)+2\mathsf{p}(x)},\quad x\geq a+\delta,

where we used the explicit expressions for
\omega_{n}
and
\sigma_{n}
from (
2.2
) and (
2.3
).
The function
\mathsf{p}
does depend on both
n
and
\mathsf{s}
, but thanks to Proposition
6.6
it remains bounded uniformly both for
\mathsf{s}\geq-\mathsf{s}_{0}
and
x\geq a+\delta
as
n\to\infty
. Therefore, for a new constant
M>0
the right-hand side above is bounded by

Mx^{\alpha}\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-n^{2m}Q(x)-2n\phi(x)}.

We integrate this bound and use that
Q(x)>0
on the positive axis, concluding that

\left|\int_{\mathsf{s}}^{\infty}\mathsf{J}_{\rm g}(u)\mathrm{d}u\right|\leq M\ee^{-\mathsf{s}}\int_{a+\delta}^{\infty}x^{\alpha}\ee^{-2n\phi(x)}\mathrm{d}x.

The function
\phi
is given in (
6.3
), and as observed from (
6.7
) it grows polynomially at
\infty
. A simple estimate then shows that this remaining integral is
\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta n})
, for some
\eta>0
.

We just concluded that

(7.18)

\int_{\mathsf{s}}^{\infty}\mathsf{J}_{\rm g}(u)\mathrm{d}u=\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-\eta n}),\quad n\to\infty,

uniformly for
\mathsf{s}\geq-\mathsf{s}_{0}
.

7.2.3.
Contributions near the soft edge

We now estimate

(7.19)

\int_{\mathsf{s}}^{\infty}\mathsf{J}_{\rm s}(u)\mathrm{d}u,\quad\text{with}\quad\mathsf{J}_{\rm s}(\mathsf{s})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{a-\delta}^{a+\delta}\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\mathbf{Y}(x\mid\mathsf{s})\right]_{21,+}\omega_{n}(x\mid\mathsf{s})\partial\log\sigma_{n}(x\mid\mathsf{s})\mathrm{d}x.

Let
\chi_{0}=\chi_{(0,a)}
be the characteristic function of the interval
(0,a)
. Recalling (
7.1
), near the soft edge at
z=a
, the unwrap of the transformations gives the relation

\mathbf{Y}_{+}(x)=\ee^{n\ell_{V}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{R}(x)\mathbf{P}_{+}(x)\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\ee^{2n\phi_{+}(x)}}{x^{\alpha}\sigma_{n}(x)}\chi_{0}(x)\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\ee^{-n(2\phi_{+}(x)-V(x))\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2},\quad a-\delta\leq x\leq a+\delta,

where
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}^{(a)}
for the rest of this section. Therefore, in the same interval,

(7.20)

\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\mathbf{Y}(x)\right]_{21,+}=\ee^{-n(2\phi_{+}(x)-V(x))}\left(\frac{\ee^{2n\phi_{+}(x)}}{x^{\alpha}\sigma_{n}(x)}\right)^{\prime}\chi_{0}(x)+\ee^{-n(2\phi_{+}(x)-V(x))}\Bigg[\left(\mathbf{I}-\frac{\chi_{0}(x)\ee^{2n\phi_{+}(x)}}{x^{\alpha}\sigma_{n}(x)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\\
\times\left(\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\mathbf{P}_{+}(x)+\mathbf{P}_{+}(x)^{-1}\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\mathbf{R}(x)\mathbf{P}_{+}(x)\right)\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{\chi_{0}(x)\ee^{2n\phi_{+}(x)}}{x^{\alpha}\sigma_{n}(x)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\Bigg]_{21}.

Using (
6.24
) and (
6.25
), as well as Theorem
6.14
and the analyticity of
\mathbf{E}^{(a)}_{1}
, we write

\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\mathbf{P}_{+}(x)+\mathbf{P}_{+}(x)^{-1}\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\mathbf{R}(x)\mathbf{P}_{+}(x)=\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\left[(\sigma_{n}(x)x^{\alpha})^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\ee^{n\widetilde{\phi}_{+}(x)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\right]+(\sigma_{n}(x)x^{\alpha})^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\ee^{-n\widetilde{\phi}_{+}(x)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\\
\times\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\left(\mathbf{A}_{+}(n^{2/3}\widetilde{\psi}(x))\right)+\mathbf{A}_{+}(n^{2/3}\widetilde{\psi}(x))^{-1}\mathcal{O}(n^{1/3})\mathbf{A}_{+}(n^{2/3}\widetilde{\psi}(x))\right]\ee^{n\widetilde{\phi}_{+}(x)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}(\sigma_{n}(x)x^{\alpha})^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}.

To bound the terms involving
\mathbf{A}
we proceed in a way similar to what we did near
z=0
. For a number
R>0
to be taken sufficiently large but fixed, define
y_{n}=y_{n}(R),x_{n}=x_{n}(R)\in(a-\delta,a+\delta)
through the relation

[y_{n},x_{n}]=\{x\in[a-\delta,a+\delta]\mid n^{2/3}|\widetilde{\psi}(x)|\leq R\}.

Choose
R>0
in such a way that the expansion (
6.22
) holds for
|\zeta|\geq R
. This is done in such a way that, on
[a-\delta,a+\delta]\setminus[y_{n},x_{n}]
we may use this expansion (
6.22
) to obtain bounds for
\mathbf{A}
, whereas along
[y_{n},x_{n}]
we may simply use that
\mathbf{A}
remains bounded.

Following this idea, we obtain the same bound in both intervals, namely

\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\mathbf{P}_{+}(x)+\mathbf{P}_{+}(x)^{-1}\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\mathbf{R}(x)\mathbf{P}_{+}(x)=\mathcal{O}(n),\quad x\in[a-\delta,a+\delta],

where we remark that we used that
\sigma_{n}(x)x^{\alpha}
remains bounded in any small neighborhood of
x=a
and the identity
n\widetilde{\phi}=\frac{2}{3}(n^{2/3}\widetilde{\psi})^{3/2}
.

We use this last estimate to bound the term in the right-most side of (
7.20
). In addition, we bound the derivative term multiplying
\xi_{0}
by observing that
\phi_{+}
is purely imaginary on
(0,a)
. As a result, we obtain the overall bound

(7.21)

\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\mathbf{Y}(x)\right]_{21,+}=\mathcal{O}\left(n\ee^{-n(2\phi_{+}(x)-V(x))}\right),\quad n\to\infty,

valid uniformly for
|x-a|\leq\delta
and
\mathsf{s}\geq\mathsf{s}_{0}
.

Using this estimate in (
7.19
), we obtain that
\mathsf{J}_{\rm s}(\mathsf{s})
is bounded by a uniform constant times

n\int_{a-\delta}^{a+\delta}x^{\alpha}\frac{\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-n^{2m}Q(x)}}{(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-n^{2m}Q(x)})^{2}}\ee^{-n\phi_{+}(x)}\mathrm{d}x\leq n\ee^{-\mathsf{s}}\int_{a-\delta}^{a+\delta}x^{\alpha}\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-n^{2m}Q(x)}\ee^{-n\phi_{+}(x)}\mathrm{d}x.

In the remaining integral, both functions
Q(x)
and
\phi_{+}(x)
are continuous on the interval of integration, with
Q
being strictly positive there. In particular, this observation implies that the whole integral is
\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta n^{2m}})
, for some
\eta>0
. Integrating this result now in the
\mathsf{s}
-variable, we obtain the estimate

(7.22)

\int_{\mathsf{s}}^{\infty}\mathsf{J}_{\rm s}(u)\mathrm{d}u=\mathcal{O}\left(\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-\eta n^{2m}}\right),\quad n\to\infty,

for some (new) value
\eta>0
, uniformly for
\mathsf{s}\geq\mathsf{s}_{0}
.

7.2.4.
Contributions from the bulk

To finalize the analysis of the terms in (
7.2
), we now analyze

(7.23)

\int_{\mathsf{s}}^{\infty}\mathsf{J}_{\rm b}(u)\mathrm{d}u,\quad\text{with}\quad\mathsf{J}_{\rm b}(\mathsf{s})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{\delta}^{a-\delta}\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\mathbf{Y}(x\mid\mathsf{s})\right]_{21,+}\omega_{n}(x\mid\mathsf{s})\partial\log\sigma_{n}(x\mid\mathsf{s})\mathrm{d}x.

For
\delta<x<a-\delta
, the unwrap of the transformations now unravels as

\mathbf{Y}_{+}(x)=\ee^{n\ell_{V}\bm{\sigma}_{3}}\mathbf{R}_{+}(x)\mathbf{G}_{+}(z)\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{x^{-\alpha}\ee^{2n\phi_{+}(x)}}{\sigma_{n}(z)}\mathbf{E}_{21}\right)\ee^{-n(\phi_{+}(z)-V(x)/2)\bm{\sigma}_{3}},

see (
7.1
).

The function
\phi_{+}
is purely imaginary along
(0,a)
. The function
\mathbf{R}
, as well as its derivative, remains bounded as
n\to\infty
. As it can be seen from Proposition
6.7
, the global parametrix (as well as its derivative) remains bounded as
n\to\infty
. This means that, when applying the identity above to estimate
\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\mathbf{Y}_{+}
, possibly growing terms come only from the exponential part
\ee^{-n(\pi_{+}(x)-V(x)/2)\bm{\sigma}_{3}}
and its derivative. With this observation in mind, we see immediately that

\left[\mathbf{\Delta}_{x}\mathbf{Y}(x)\right]_{21,+}=\mathcal{O}(n\ee^{nV(x)}),\quad n\to\infty,

uniformly for
x\in(\delta,a-\delta)
and uniformly for
\mathsf{s}\geq\mathsf{s}_{0}
. This estimate is not necessarily optimal, but sufficient for our purposes.

Proceeding in exactly the same way as we did from (
7.21
) onwards, we obtain

(7.24)

\int_{\mathsf{s}}^{\infty}\mathsf{J}_{\rm b}(u)\mathrm{d}u=\mathcal{O}\left(\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-\eta n^{2m}}\right),\quad n\to\infty,

for some
\eta>0
, uniformly for
\mathsf{s}\geq\mathsf{s}_{0}
.

7.2.5.
Summary of contributions and conclusion of the proof of Theorem
2.6

Summarizing, we combine (
7.15
), (
7.18
), (
7.22
) and (
7.24
) into (
7.2
), obtaining

\log\mathsf{L}_{n}^{Q}(\mathsf{s})=-\int_{\mathsf{s}}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\mathsf{K}_{\alpha}(-\zeta,-\zeta\mid u)\frac{\ee^{-\mathsf{h}_{\infty}(\zeta\mid u)}}{1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}_{\infty}(\zeta\mid u)}}\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}u+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\ee^{-\mathsf{s}}}{n}\right),\quad n\to\infty,

where the error term is uniform for
\mathsf{s}\geq\mathsf{s}_{0}
with any fixed
\mathsf{s}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. The proof of Theorem
2.6
is completed by making the change of variables
\zeta\mapsto-\zeta
and using that
(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{h}_{\infty}(\zeta\mid\mathsf{s})})^{-1}=\sigma_{\mathbf{\Phi}}\left(\frac{4}{\mathsf{x}^{2}}\zeta\mid\mathsf{s}\right)
.

Appendix A
The Bessel parametrix

The Bessel parametrix
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}
is a solution to a canonical RHP which depends parametrically on
\alpha>-1
, and which was first introduced in
[
40
]
and since then has been used widely as a model problem in the asymptotic analysis of RHPs.

Set
\Gamma_{\pm}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(\ee^{\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i}/3}\infty,0]
,
\Gamma_{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(-\infty,0]
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Gamma_{0}\cup\Gamma_{+}\cup\Gamma_{-}
, which is consistent with (
3.1
) for the choice
m=1
. Introduce

(A.1)

\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha}^{(\text{Bes})}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{\alpha}(2z^{1/2})&\dfrac{\mathrm{i}}{\pi}K_{\alpha}(2z^{1/2})\\
2\pi\mathrm{i}z^{1/2}I_{\alpha}^{\prime}(2z^{1/2})&-2z^{1/2}K_{\alpha}^{\prime}(2z^{1/2})\end{pmatrix}\begin{cases}\mathbf{I},&|\arg z|<\frac{2\pi}{3},\\
(\mathbf{I}\mp\ee^{\pm\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}\mathbf{E}_{21}),&\frac{2\pi}{3}<\pm\arg z<\pi,\end{cases}

where
I_{\alpha}(z)
and
K_{\alpha}(z)
denote the modified Bessel functions and the principal branch is taken for
z^{1/2}
.

Following
[
40
, Pages 365–368, in particular Theorem 6.3, and also Equation (8.4)]
, the matrix
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}
is the solution to the following RHP.

RHP A.1
.

(i)

\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(z)
is defined and analytic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0]
.

(ii)

For
z\in\Gamma\setminus\{0\}
, we have

(A.2)

\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha,+}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(z)=\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha,-}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(z)\mathbf{J}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(z),\quad\text{with}\quad\mathbf{J}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}+\ee^{\pm\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}\mathbf{E}_{21},&z\in\Gamma^{\pm},\\
\mathbf{E}_{12}-\mathbf{E}_{21},&z\in\Gamma_{0}.\end{cases}

(iii)

With
(\alpha,0):=1
,

(\alpha,k)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{(4\alpha^{2}-1)(4\alpha^{2}-3^{2})\cdots(4\alpha^{2}-(2k-1)^{2})}{2^{2k}k!},\quad k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},

and

(A.3)

a_{k}(\alpha)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{(\alpha,k-1)}{4^{k}k}\left(\alpha^{2}+\frac{k}{2}-\frac{1}{4}\right),\quad b_{k}(\alpha)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{(\alpha,k-1)}{4^{k}}\left(k-\frac{1}{2}\right),\quad k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},

the function
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}
admits the following asymptotic expansion as
z\to\infty
,

(A.4)

\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(z)\sim(2\pi)^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}z^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\left(\mathbf{I}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{z^{k/2}}\left(a_{k}(\alpha)\mathbf{I}+b_{k}(\alpha)\bm{\sigma}_{2}\right)\bm{\sigma}_{3}^{k}\right)\ee^{2z^{1/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}},

where
\bm{\sigma}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathrm{i}\\
\mathrm{i}&0\end{pmatrix}
and we choose the principal branches of
z^{1/4}
and
z^{1/2}
.

(iv)

As
z\to 0
we have

(A.5)

\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha}^{(\text{Bes})}(z)=\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha,0}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(z)z^{\alpha\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathsf{a}(z)\mathbf{E}_{12}\right)(\mathbf{I}-(\chi_{\mathcal{S}_{+}}(\zeta)\ee^{\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha}-\chi_{\mathcal{S}_{-}}(\zeta)\ee^{-\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha})\mathbf{E}_{21}),

where
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha,0}^{(\text{Bes})}(z)
is analytic in a neighborhood of the origin, we choose principal branch for
z^{\alpha/2}
, and
\mathsf{a}(z)=\mathsf{a}_{\alpha}(z)
is as in (
3.7
).

The local behavior in (
A.5
) is slightly more precise than the one described in
[
40
, Equations (6.19)–(6.21)]
. Nevertheless, applying Proposition
3.2
with the choice
\sigma\equiv 1
, we get that the behavior from
[
40
]
implies (
A.5
). Since we also need the value
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha,0}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(0)
at the origin, we now verify the behavior (
A.5
) directly from (
A.1
).

The modified Bessel function
I_{\alpha}(w)
is defined through
[
32
, (10.25.2)]

I_{\alpha}(w)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\left(\frac{w}{2}\right)^{\alpha}F_{\alpha}(w^{2}/4),\quad\text{where}\quad F_{\alpha}(w)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{w^{k}}{k!\Gamma(\alpha+k+1)}.

Observe that
F_{\alpha}
is an entire function for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. From this expression for
I_{\alpha}
, we compute

(A.6)

I_{\alpha}(2z^{1/2})=z^{\alpha/2}F_{\alpha}(z)\qquad\text{and}\qquad z^{1/2}I_{\alpha}^{\prime}(2z^{1/2})=z^{\alpha/2}\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}F_{\alpha}(z)+zF_{\alpha}^{\prime}(z)\right).

Using there relations and (
A.1
) and (
A.5
), we are able to compute the matrix function
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha,0}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}
explicitly.

For any
\alpha>-1
, we compute that for
z>0
the first column of
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha,0}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}
is given by

(A.7)

\left(\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha,0}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(z)\right)_{11}=z^{-\alpha/2}\left(\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(z)\right)_{11}=F_{\alpha}(z)=z^{-\alpha/2}I_{\alpha}(2z^{1/2})

and

(A.8)

\left(\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha,0}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(z)\right)_{21}=z^{-\alpha/2}\left(\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(z)\right)_{21}=\pi\mathrm{i}\alpha F_{\alpha}(z)+2\pi\mathrm{i}zF^{\prime}_{\alpha}(z)=2\pi\mathrm{i}z^{(1-\alpha)/2}I_{\alpha}^{\prime}(2z^{1/2}).

By analytic continuation, these identities extend from
z>0
to
z\in\mathbb{C}
.

The second column of
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha,0}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}
could be described similarly, but we will not need it so we skip the details.

As a consequence, it is readily seen from (
A.1
) and (
A.5
) that

(A.9)

\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha,0}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(0)=\begin{cases}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}&\mathrm{i}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha)}{2\pi}\\
\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}&\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}{2}\end{pmatrix},&\alpha\neq 0,\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\pi}\log 2\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},&\alpha=0.\end{cases}

For us, we need
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}
to construct the particular solution
\mathbf{\Psi}(\cdot\mid\mathsf{h}=+\infty)=\mathbf{\Psi}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}
of the model problem RHP
3.1
. Setting

\mathbf{\Psi}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(\mathbf{I}+\mathrm{i}(a_{1}(\alpha)+b_{1}(\alpha))\mathbf{E}_{21})(2\pi)^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(z),

where
a_{1}(\alpha)
and
b_{1}(\alpha)
are given in (
A.3
), the conditions in RHP
A.1
imply that indeed
\mathbf{\Psi}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}=\mathbf{\Psi}(\cdot\mid\mathsf{h}=+\infty)
. For the record, notice that

(A.10)

\left(\mathbf{\Psi}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(z)\right)_{j1}=(2\pi)^{(-1)^{j+1}/2}\left(\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(z)\right)_{j1},\quad j=1,2,

and we also state that (
A.4
) yields

(A.11)

\mathbf{\Psi}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(z)\sim\left(\mathbf{I}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{z^{k}}\mathbf{\Psi}_{\infty,k}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(\alpha)\right)z^{-\bm{\sigma}_{3}/4}\mathbf{U}_{0}\ee^{2z^{1/2}\bm{\sigma}_{3}},\quad z\to\infty,

with

(A.12)

\mathbf{\Psi}_{\infty,k}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(\alpha)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(\mathbf{I}+\mathrm{i}(a_{1}(\alpha)+b_{1}(\alpha))\mathbf{E}_{21})\begin{pmatrix}a_{2k}(\alpha)-b_{2k}(\alpha)&\mathrm{i}(a_{2k-1}(\alpha)-b_{2k-1}(\alpha))\\
-\mathrm{i}(a_{2k+1}(\alpha)+b_{2k+1}(\alpha))&a_{2k}(\alpha)+b_{2k}(\alpha)\end{pmatrix},\quad k\geq 1.

In particular,

(A.13)

\left(\mathbf{\Psi}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(z)\right)_{11}=\frac{z^{-1/4}}{\sqrt{2}}\left(1+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1})\right)\ee^{2z^{1/2}},\quad\left(\mathbf{\Psi}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(z)\right)_{21}=\frac{\mathrm{i}z^{1/4}}{\sqrt{2}}\left(1+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1})\right)\ee^{2z^{1/2}},\quad z\to\infty,

and

(A.14)

\left(\mathbf{\Psi}_{\infty,1}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(\alpha)\right)_{12}=\frac{\mathrm{i}(4\alpha^{2}-1)}{16}.

Furthermore, the local expansion (
3.9
) holds with, as said,
\sigma(z)\equiv 1
and

\mathbf{\Psi}_{0}(z)=\mathbf{\Psi}_{0,\alpha}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(\mathbf{I}+\mathrm{i}(a_{1}(\alpha)+b_{1}(\alpha))\mathbf{E}_{21})(2\pi)^{\bm{\sigma}_{3}/2}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\alpha,0}^{\mathrm{(Bes)}}(z).

Appendix B
Asymptotic analysis for a class of integrals

Consider a function
q
holomorphic on a neighborhood of the origin, with expansion

q(z)=q_{0}z^{m}(1+\mathcal{O}(z)),\quad z\to 0,

for some
m\geq 1
and
q_{0}>0
. Assume in addition that
q>0
on an interval of the form
[0,\delta]
,
\delta>0
. For a number
\beta>-1
and a smooth function
f
on
[0,\delta]
with
f(0)\neq 0
, introduce

\mathsf{I}_{t}(\mathsf{s})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{0}^{\delta}x^{\beta}f(x)\log(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-tq(x)})\mathrm{d}x,\quad t>0,\quad\mathsf{s}\in\mathbb{R}.

We assume that
x^{\beta}f(x)\in L^{1}[0,\delta]
. We are interested in the behavior of
\mathsf{I}_{t}(\mathsf{s})
when
t\to\infty
simultaneously with
\mathsf{s}\to+\infty
. For the next result, set

\mathsf{F}_{\beta}(\mathsf{s})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{\beta}\log(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-x})\mathrm{d}x,\quad\mathsf{s}\in\mathbb{R},\quad\beta>-1.

Proposition B.1
.

(i)

For
\mathsf{s}>0
and any
\beta>-1
, the identity

\mathsf{F}_{\beta}(\mathsf{s})=-\beta\Gamma(\beta)\Li_{\beta+2}(-\ee^{-\mathsf{s}})

holds, where
\Li_{\beta}
stands for the polylogarithms. Furthermore, for any
\mathsf{s}_{0}>0
, the estimate

\mathsf{F}_{\beta}(\mathsf{s})=\beta\Gamma(\beta)\ee^{-\mathsf{s}}+\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-2\mathsf{s}}),\quad\mathsf{s}\to+\infty,

is valid uniformly for
\mathsf{s}\geq\mathsf{s}_{0}
.

(ii)

For any
\mathsf{s}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, the estimate

\mathsf{I}_{t}(\mathsf{s})=\frac{f(0)}{mq_{0}^{\frac{\beta+1}{m}}}\frac{1}{t^{\frac{\beta+1}{m}}}\left(\mathsf{F}_{\frac{\beta+1}{m}-1}(\mathsf{s})+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)\right),\quad t\to\infty,

holds uniformly for
\mathsf{s}\geq\mathsf{s}_{0}
.

Notice that the function
\mathsf{s}\mapsto\mathsf{F}_{\beta}(\mathsf{s})
is bounded on any interval of the form
[M,\infty)
, so the estimate (ii) indeed singles out the leading term in
\mathsf{I}_{t}(\mathsf{s})
on the range
\mathsf{s}\in(\mathsf{s}_{0},+\infty)
.

Proof.

For the identity between
\mathsf{F}_{\beta}
and the polylog, we use the assumption
\mathsf{s}>0
to expand the log in series and interchange the order of summation and integration, obtaining

\mathsf{F}_{\beta}(\mathsf{s})=-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\left(-\ee^{-\mathsf{s}}\right)^{k}}{k}\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{\beta}\ee^{-kx}\mathrm{d}x=-\Gamma(\beta+1)\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\left(-\ee^{-\mathsf{s}}\right)^{k}}{k^{\beta+2}},

where the last identity follows performing the change of variables
kx=u
in the integration. This last series is precisely the series expansion of
\Li_{\beta+2}(-\ee^{-\mathsf{s}})
, and it also yields the claimed asymptotic estimate for
\mathsf{F}_{\beta}
.

To prove (ii), we start with the simpler case when
f(x)\equiv 1
and
q(x)\equiv x
. In this case, we change variables
tx=v
and obtain

\int_{0}^{\delta}x^{\beta}\log(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-tx})\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{t^{\beta+1}}\mathsf{F}_{\beta}(\mathsf{s})-\frac{1}{t^{\beta+1}}\int_{\delta t}^{\infty}v^{\beta}\log(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-v})\mathrm{d}v.

To estimate the last integral, we use the inequality
\log(1+u)\leq u
, which is valid for any
u\geq 0
, and obtain

\int_{\delta t}^{\infty}v^{\beta}\log(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-v})\mathrm{d}v\leq\ee^{-\mathsf{s}_{0}}\int_{t\delta}^{\infty}v^{\beta}\ee^{-v}\mathrm{d}v\leq\ee^{-\mathsf{s}_{0}}\int_{0}^{\infty}v^{\beta}\ee^{-v}\mathrm{d}v.

The last integral above is independent of
t
, and shows that

(B.1)

\int_{0}^{\delta}x^{\beta}\log(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-tx})\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{t^{\beta+1}}\mathsf{F}_{\beta}(\mathsf{s})+\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta t}).

For the general case, we fix a positive value
\delta^{\prime}<\delta
for which
q
is a smooth bijection on
[0,\delta^{\prime}]
and
f
is smooth on
[0,\delta^{\prime}]
, and split the integral

\mathsf{I}_{t}(\mathsf{s})=\int_{0}^{\delta^{\prime}}x^{\beta}f(x)\log(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-tq(x)})\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\delta^{\prime}}^{\delta}x^{\beta}f(x)\log(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-tq(x)})\mathrm{d}x.

Since
q
is strictly positive on
[\delta^{\prime},\delta]
, it has a minimum therein, say
q(x)\geq q_{1}>0
. Proceeding as before, it is immediate to see that the last integral decays exponentially fast in
t
, uniformly for
\mathsf{s}\geq\mathsf{s}_{0}
, and therefore

\mathsf{I}_{t}(\mathsf{s})=\int_{0}^{\delta^{\prime}}x^{\beta}f(x)\log(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-tq(x)})\mathrm{d}x+\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta t}),\quad t\to\infty,

where
\eta>0
is uniform for
\mathsf{s}\geq\mathsf{s}_{0}
. For the last integral, we change variables
tq(x)=u
, and with
\widetilde{q}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=q^{-1}
being the functional inverse of
q
on
[0,\delta^{\prime}]
we obtain

\int_{0}^{\delta^{\prime}}x^{\beta}f(x)\log(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-tq(x)})\mathrm{d}x=\int_{0}^{q(\delta^{\prime})}\widetilde{q}(u)^{\beta}f\left(\widetilde{q}(u)\right)(\widetilde{q})^{\prime}(u)\log(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-tu})\mathrm{d}u.

A local analysis near the origin shows that

\widetilde{q}(u)^{\beta}f\left(\widetilde{q}(u)\right)(\widetilde{q})^{\prime}(u)=\frac{f(0)}{mq_{0}^{(\beta+1)/m}}u^{(\beta+1-m)/m}+R(u)

where the error term
R
satisfies
|R(u)|\leq R_{0}u^{(\beta+1)/m}
,
0\leq u\leq\delta^{\prime}
, for some positive constant
R_{0}
that depends on
f,\widetilde{q},\beta
but it is independent of
t,\mathsf{s}
. Therefore,

\int_{0}^{\delta^{\prime}}x^{\beta}f(x)\log(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-tq(x)})\mathrm{d}x=\\
\frac{f(0)}{mq_{0}^{(\beta+1)/m}}\int_{0}^{q(\delta^{\prime})}u^{(\beta+1-m)/m}\log(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-tu})\mathrm{d}u+\int_{0}^{q(\delta^{\prime})}R(u)\log(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-tu})\mathrm{d}u.

From (
B.1
), we obtain the estimates

\int_{0}^{q(\delta^{\prime})}u^{(\beta+1-m)/m}\log(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-tu})\mathrm{d}u=\frac{1}{t^{\frac{\beta+1}{m}}}\mathsf{F}_{\frac{\beta+1-m}{m}}(\mathsf{s})+\mathcal{O}(\ee^{-\eta t})

and

\int_{0}^{q(\delta^{\prime})}R(u)\log(1+\ee^{-\mathsf{s}-tu})\mathrm{d}u=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{t^{\frac{\beta+1}{m}+1}}\mathsf{F}_{\frac{\beta+1}{m}}(\mathsf{s})+\ee^{-\eta t}\right),

as
t\to\infty
, completing the proof.
∎
